The four pācittiya offenses not shareable with another are: Those spoken of thus, 'Because of this very reason, it is a non-shareable pācittiya,' are these four: the training rule on arranging a sleeping place by encroaching, the training rule on 'Come, friend, to a village or town,' intentionally causing remorse, and standing to eavesdrop. The four permissions by the bhikkhus are: The permissions that are distinct from the thirteen permissions that have come down thus: 'If a bhikkhu should dwell apart from his three robes even for one night, except by the bhikkhu's permission...'; '...should have a new felt rug made, except by the bhikkhu's permission...'; '...if he should dwell apart beyond that, except by the bhikkhu's permission...'; '...should inform one not fully ordained of a serious offense, except by the bhikkhu's permission.' In the tetrad concerning the sick: a sick person who, out of greed, requests another medicine when there is something to be done with a different medicine, incurs an offense; one who is not sick who requests medicine for a matter that does not require medicine incurs an offense; both incur offenses such as false speech; both do not incur a non-common offense. The rest is clear everywhere.

Catukkavāravaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The explanation of the chapter on tetrads is finished.

Pañcakavāravaṇṇanā

Commentary on the Section of Fives

325. Pañcakesu [P 7.1334][V 0.0173] pañca puggalā niyatāti ānantariyānamevetaṃ gahaṇaṃ. Pañca chedanakā āpattiyo nāma pamāṇātikkante mañcapīṭhe nisīdanakaṇḍuppaṭicchādivassikasāṭikāsu sugatacīvare ca veditabbā. Pañcahākārehīti alajjitā, aññāṇatā, kukkuccappakatatā, akappiye kappiyasaññitā, kappiye akappiyasaññitāti imehi pañcahi. Pañca āpattiyo musāvādapaccayāti pārājikathullaccayadukkaṭasaṅghādisesapācittiyā. Anāmantacāroti “santaṃ bhikkhuṃ anāpucchā purebhattaṃ pacchābhattaṃ kulesu cārittaṃ āpajjeyyā”ti imassa āpucchitvā cārassa abhāvo. Anadhiṭṭhānanti “gaṇabhojane aññatra samayā”ti vuttaṃ [M 0.0173] samayaṃ adhiṭṭhahitvā bhojanaṃ adhiṭṭhānaṃ nāma; tathā akaraṇaṃ anadhiṭṭhānaṃ. Avikappanā nāma yā paramparabhojane vikappanā vuttā, tassā akaraṇaṃ. Imāni hi pañca piṇḍapātikassa dhutaṅgeneva paṭikkhittāni. Ussaṅkitaparisaṅkitoti ye passanti, ye suṇanti, tehi ussaṅkito ceva parisaṅkito ca. Api akuppadhammo khīṇāsavopi samāno, tasmā agocarā pariharitabbā. Na hi etesu sandissamāno ayasato vā garahato vā muccati. Sosānikanti susāne patitakaṃ. Pāpaṇikanti āpaṇadvāre patitakaṃ. Thūpacīvaranti vammikaṃ parikkhipitvā balikammakataṃ. Ābhisekikanti nahānaṭṭhāne vā rañño abhisekaṭṭhāne vā chaḍḍitacīvaraṃ. Bhatapaṭiyābhatanti susānaṃ netvā puna ānītakaṃ. Pañca mahācorā uttarimanussadhamme vuttā.

325. In the groups of five, the statement 'five persons are fixed' is a designation for the very same acts with immediate results. The five offenses involving cutting are to be understood in relation to exceeding the measure for beds and benches, sitting cloths, itch-covering cloths, rainy-season robes, and the Sugata’s robe. By 'five reasons' is meant: shamelessness, ignorance, being overcome by scrupulousness, perceiving the unallowable as allowable, and perceiving the allowable as unallowable. The five offenses conditioned by false speech are: pārājika, thullaccaya, dukkaṭa, saṅghādisesa, and pācittiya. 'Going about without asking leave' means the absence of going about after having asked leave, as in the rule: 'should a bhikkhu, without asking an available bhikkhu, go among families before or after the meal.' 'Non-determination' means not making a determination; in the context of 'at a group meal, except at the proper time,' eating after having determined the time is called 'determination,' and not doing so is 'non-determination.' ‘Non-designation’ means not performing the designation mentioned in the context of consecutive meals. Indeed, these five are rejected by the very ascetic practice of one who eats only alms-food. 'Suspected and distrusted' means being suspected and distrusted by those who see and those who hear. Even though one is an Arahant with an unshakable nature, improper resorts should be avoided. For indeed, one seen in them is not freed from ill-repute or blame. 'A charnel-ground robe' is one fallen in a charnel ground. 'A shop robe' is one fallen at a shop's door. 'A stupa-robe' is a cloth wrapped around an anthill as an offering. 'A consecration robe' is a robe discarded at a bathing place or at a king's consecration site. 'A robe brought back' is one taken to a charnel ground and then brought back. The five great thieves are mentioned in the section on superhuman states.

Pañcāpattiyo kāyato samuṭṭhantīti paṭhamena āpattisamuṭṭhānena pañca āpattiyo āpajjati, “bhikkhu kappiyasaññī saññācikāya kuṭiṃ karotī”ti evaṃ antarapeyyāle vuttāpattiyo. Pañca āpattiyo kāyato ca vācato cāti tatiyena āpattisamuṭṭhānena pañca āpattiyo āpajjati, “bhikkhu kappiyasaññī saṃvidahitvā kuṭiṃ karotī”ti evaṃ tattheva vuttā āpattiyo. Desanāgāminiyoti ṭhapetvā pārājikañca saṅghādisesañca avasesā.

'Five offenses arise from the body'—thus, by the first origin of offenses, one commits five offenses, such as those mentioned in the intermediate elided passage: 'A bhikkhu, perceiving it as allowable, builds a hut by requesting for himself.' 'Five offenses arise from both body and speech'—thus, by the third origin of offenses, one commits five offenses, such as those mentioned in that same place: 'A bhikkhu, perceiving it as allowable, builds a hut after arranging.' 'Those absolved by confession' are the remaining offenses, setting aside the pārājika and saṅghādisesa.

Pañca kammānīti tajjanīyaniyassapabbājanīyapaṭisāraṇīyāni cattāri ukkhepanīyañca tividhampi ekanti pañca. Yāvatatiyake pañcāti ukkhittānuvattikāya bhikkhuniyā yāvatatiyaṃ samanubhāsanāya appaṭinissajjantiyā pārājikaṃ thullaccayaṃ dukkaṭanti tisso[P 7.1335], bhedakānuvattakādisamanubhāsanāsu saṅghādiseso, pāpikāya diṭṭhiyā appaṭinissagge pācittiyaṃ. Adinnanti [V 0.0174] aññena adinnaṃ. Aviditanti paṭiggaṇhāmīti cetanāya abhāvena aviditaṃ. Akappiyanti pañcahi samaṇakappehi akappiyakataṃ; yaṃ vā panaññampi akappiyamaṃsaṃ akappiyabhojanaṃ. Akatātirittanti pavāretvā atirittaṃ akataṃ. Samajjadānanti naṭasamajjādidānaṃ. Usabhadānanti gogaṇassa antare usabhavissajjanaṃ. Cittakammadānanti āvāsaṃ kāretvā tattha cittakammaṃ kāretuṃ vaṭṭati. Idaṃ pana paṭibhānacittakammadānaṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ. Imāni hi pañca kiñcāpi lokassa puññasammatāni, atha kho apuññāni akusalāniyeva[M 0.0174]. Uppannaṃ paṭibhānanti ettha paṭibhānanti kathetukamyatā vuccati. Ime pañca duppaṭivinodayāti na supaṭivinodayā; upāyena pana kāraṇena anurūpāhi paccavekkhanāanusāsanādīhi sakkā paṭivinodetunti attho.

‘Five formal acts’ are the four—the act of censure, the act of requiring dependence, the act of banishment, and the act of reconciliation—and also the three kinds of suspension, which count as one, making five in total. ‘Five up to the third time’ refers to the three offenses—pārājika, thullaccaya, and dukkaṭa—for a bhikkhunī who follows one who has been suspended, if she does not desist after being admonished up to the third time; a saṅghādisesa for those who follow schismatics and others, if they do not desist after being admonished; and a pācittiya for not giving up a wrong view. ‘Not given’ means not given by another. ‘Not known’ means not known due to the absence of the intention to receive. ‘Unallowable’ means made unallowable by the five monastic usages; or any other unallowable meat or unallowable food. ‘Leftover not made allowable’ means that after one has been invited to take no more, what is leftover has not been made allowable. ‘Giving for festivals’ means giving for festivals of dancers and the like. ‘Giving a bull’ means releasing a bull among a herd of cows. ‘Giving of art’ means it is permissible to have a dwelling built and then commission artwork there; this, however, refers to giving artistic work out of inspiration. Indeed, these five, though considered meritorious by the world, are in fact demeritorious and unwholesome. In ‘arisen inspiration,’ ‘inspiration’ means the desire to speak. ‘These five are hard to dispel’ means they are not easily dispelled; yet they can be dispelled by means of appropriate reflection, instruction, and other suitable methods—this is the meaning.

Sakacittaṃ pasīdatīti ettha imāni vatthūni – kaṭaandhakāravāsī phussadevatthero kira cetiyaṅgaṇaṃ sammajjitvā ekaṃsaṃ uttarāsaṅgaṃ karitvā sinduvārakusumasanthatamiva samavippakiṇṇavālikaṃ cetiyaṅgaṇaṃ olokento buddhārammaṇaṃ pītipāmojjaṃ uppādetvā aṭṭhāsi. Tasmiṃ khaṇe māro pabbatapāde nibbattakāḷamakkaṭo viya hutvā cetiyaṅgaṇe gomayaṃ vippakiranto gato. Thero nāsakkhi arahattaṃ pāpuṇituṃ, sammajjitvā agamāsi. Dutiyadivasepi jaraggavo hutvā tādisameva vippakāraṃ akāsi. Tatiyadivase vaṅkapādaṃ manussattabhāvaṃ nimminitvā pādena parikasanto agamāsi. Thero “evarūpo bībhacchapuriso samantā yojanappamāṇesu gocaragāmesu natthi, siyā nu kho māro”ti cintetvā “mārosi tva”nti āha. “Āma, bhante, māromhi, na dāni te vañcetuṃ asakkhi”nti. “Diṭṭhapubbo tayā tathāgato”ti? “Āma, diṭṭhapubbo”ti. “Māro nāma mahānubhāvo hoti, iṅgha tāva buddhassa bhagavato attabhāvasadisaṃ attabhāvaṃ nimmināhī”ti? “Na sakkā, bhante, tādisaṃ rūpaṃ nimminituṃ; apica kho pana taṃsarikkhakaṃ patirūpakaṃ nimminissāmī”ti sakabhāvaṃ vijahitvā buddharūpasadisena attabhāvena aṭṭhāsi[P 7.1336]. Thero māraṃ oloketvā “ayaṃ tāva sarāgadosamoho evaṃ sobhati, kathaṃ nu kho bhagavā na sobhati sabbaso vītarāgadosamoho”ti buddhārammaṇaṃ pītiṃ paṭilabhitvā vipassanaṃ vaḍḍhetvā arahattaṃ pāpuṇi. Māro “vañcitomhi tayā, bhante”ti āha. Theropi “kiṃ atthi jaramāra, tādisaṃ vañcetu”nti āha. Lokantaravihārepi datto nāma daharabhikkhu cetiyaṅgaṇaṃ sammajjitvā olokento odātakasiṇaṃ paṭilabhi. Aṭṭha samāpattiyo nibbattesi. Tato vipassanaṃ vaḍḍhetvā phalattayaṃ sacchākāsi.

‘His own mind becomes serene’—here are the stories: It is said that the Elder Phussadeva, who lived in Kaṭaandhakāra, swept the shrine courtyard, draped his upper robe over one shoulder, and stood gazing at the shrine courtyard, which had sand evenly scattered as if strewn with sinduvāra flowers. He generated joy and gladness with the Buddha as his object. At that moment, Māra, having become like a black monkey born at the foot of a mountain, went about scattering cow dung in the shrine courtyard. The elder was unable to attain arahantship; he swept again and departed. On the second day, Māra, having become an old ox, caused the same kind of disturbance. On the third day, he created a human form with a crooked foot and went about dragging his foot. The elder thought, “Such a repulsive person does not exist in the surrounding alms-round villages within a yojana—could this be Māra?” and said, “Are you Māra?” “Yes, venerable sir, I am Māra. I have not been able to deceive you.” “Have you seen the Tathāgata before?” “Yes, I have seen him before.” “Māra is said to be of great power. Come now, create a form similar to the Blessed One’s own body.” “It is not possible, venerable sir, to create such a form; but I will create a likeness similar to him.” Abandoning his own form, he stood there in a body resembling the Buddha’s form. The elder, looking at Māra, thought, “If this one, who still has lust, hatred, and delusion, shines so brightly, how could the Blessed One, who is entirely free from lust, hatred, and delusion, not shine?” Having gained joy with the Buddha as his object, he developed insight and attained arahantship. Māra said, “Venerable sir, I have been deceived by you.” The elder also said, “Old Māra, what is there to deceive one such as you?” Also in the Lokantara Monastery, a young bhikkhu named Datta, while sweeping the shrine courtyard and gazing at it, attained the white kasiṇa. He produced the eight attainments. Thereafter, having developed insight, he realized the three lower fruits.

Paracittaṃ [V 0.0175] pasīdatīti ettha imāni vatthūni – tisso nāma daharabhikkhu jambukolacetiyaṅgaṇaṃ sammajjitvā saṅkārachaḍḍaniṃ hatthena gahetvāva aṭṭhāsi[M 0.0175]. Tasmiṃ khaṇe tissadattatthero nāma nāvāto oruyha cetiyaṅgaṇaṃ olokento bhāvitacittena sammaṭṭhaṭṭhānanti ñatvā pañhāsahassaṃ pucchi, itaro sabbaṃ vissajjesi. Aññatarasmimpi vihāre thero cetiyaṅgaṇaṃ sammajjitvā vattaṃ paricchindi. Yonakavisayato cetiyavandakā cattāro therā āgantvā cetiyaṅgaṇaṃ disvā anto appavisitvā dvāreyeva ṭhatvā eko thero aṭṭha kappe anussari, eko soḷasa, eko vīsati, eko tiṃsa kappe anussari.

‘The mind of another becomes serene’—here are the stories: A young bhikkhu named Tissa, having swept the courtyard of the Jambukola shrine, stood holding the rubbish basket in his hand. At that moment, an elder named Tissadatta, descending from a boat and observing the shrine courtyard, knew, ‘This is a place swept by one with a developed mind,’ and asked a thousand questions. The other answered them all. In another monastery as well, an elder, having swept the shrine courtyard, completed his duty. Four elders, who were shrine-worshippers from the Yona country, arrived and, seeing the shrine courtyard, did not enter but stood at the gate. One elder recollected eight eons, one recollected sixteen, one recollected twenty, and one recollected thirty eons.

Devatā attamanā hontīti ettha idaṃ vatthu – ekasmiṃ kira vihāre eko bhikkhu cetiyaṅgaṇañca bodhiyaṅgaṇañca sammajjitvā nahāyituṃ gato. Devatā “imassa vihārassa katakālato paṭṭhāya evaṃ vattaṃ pūretvā sammaṭṭhapubbo bhikkhu natthī”ti pasannacittā pupphahatthā aṭṭhaṃsu. Thero āgantvā “kataragāmavāsikātthā”ti āha. “Bhante, idheva vasāma, imassa vihārassa katakālato paṭṭhāya evaṃ vattaṃ pūretvā sammaṭṭhapubbo bhikkhu natthīti tumhākaṃ, bhante, vatte pasīditvā pupphahatthā ṭhitāmhā”ti devatā āhaṃsu.

‘The deities are delighted’—here is the story: It is said that in a certain monastery, a bhikkhu, having swept the shrine courtyard and the Bodhi-tree courtyard, went to bathe. The deities, with pleased minds, thought, “Since the time this monastery was made, there has not been a bhikkhu who has fulfilled his duty and swept like this,” and they stood holding flowers. When the elder returned, he asked, “Of which village are you inhabitants?” The deities replied, “Venerable sir, we dwell right here. Since the time this monastery was made, there has not been a bhikkhu who has fulfilled his duty and swept like this. Being pleased with your duty, venerable sir, we are standing holding flowers.”

Pāsādikasaṃvattanikanti ettha idaṃ vatthu – ekaṃ kira amaccaputtaṃ abhayattherañca ārabbha ayaṃ kathā udapādi “kiṃ nu kho amaccaputto pāsādiko, abhayattheroti ubhopi ne ekasmiṃ ṭhāne olokessāmā”ti. Ñātakā amaccaputtaṃ alaṅkaritvā mahācetiyaṃ vandāpessāmāti agamaṃsu. Theramātāpi pāsādikaṃ cīvaraṃ kāretvā puttassa pahiṇi, “putto me kese chindāpetvā imaṃ cīvaraṃ pārupitvā bhikkhusaṅghaparivuto mahācetiyaṃ vandatū”ti. Amaccaputto [P 7.1337] ñātiparivuto pācīnadvārena cetiyaṅgaṇaṃ āruḷho, abhayatthero bhikkhusaṅghaparivuto dakkhiṇadvārena cetiyaṅgaṇaṃ āruhitvā cetiyaṅgaṇe tena saddhiṃ samāgantvā āha – “kiṃ tvaṃ, āvuso, mahallakattherassa sammaṭṭhaṭṭhāne kacavaraṃ chaḍḍetvā mayā saddhiṃ yugaggāhaṃ gaṇhāsī”ti. Atītattabhāve kira abhayatthero mahallakatthero nāma hutvā gocaragāme cetiyaṅgaṇaṃ sammajji, amaccaputto mahāupāsako hutvā sammaṭṭhaṭṭhāne kacavaraṃ gahetvā chaḍḍesi.

‘Conducive to admiration’—here is the story: It is said that this talk arose concerning a minister’s son and the Elder Abhaya: “Who is more admirable, the minister’s son or the Elder Abhaya? Let us observe them both in the same place.” The relatives adorned the minister’s son and went, intending, “We will have him pay homage to the Great Shrine.” The elder’s mother also had an admirable robe made and sent it to her son with the message: “Let my son have his hair cut, don this robe, and, surrounded by the community of bhikkhus, pay homage to the Great Shrine.” The minister’s son, surrounded by his relatives, ascended to the shrine courtyard through the eastern gate. The Elder Abhaya, surrounded by the community of bhikkhus, ascended to the shrine courtyard through the southern gate and, meeting the minister's son there, said: “Friend, having cleared away rubbish in a place swept by a senior elder, do you now compete with me?” It is said that in a past existence, the Elder Abhaya had been a senior elder and had swept the shrine courtyard in an alms-round village; the minister’s son had been a great lay devotee and had taken the rubbish from that swept place and discarded it.

Satthusāsanaṃ kataṃ hotīti idaṃ sammajjanavattaṃ nāma buddhehi vaṇṇitaṃ, tasmā taṃ karontena satthusāsanaṃ kataṃ hoti. Tatridaṃ vatthu – āyasmā [M 0.0176] kira sāriputto himavantaṃ gantvā ekasmiṃ [V 0.0176] pabbhāre asammajjitvāva nirodhaṃ samāpajjitvā nisīdi. Bhagavā āvajjanto therassa asammajjitvā nisinnabhāvaṃ ñatvā ākāsena gantvā therassa purato asammaṭṭhaṭṭhāne pādāni dassetvā paccāgañchi. Thero samāpattito vuṭṭhito bhagavato pādāni disvā balavahirottappaṃ paccupaṭṭhāpetvā jaṇṇukehi patiṭṭhāya “asammajjitvā nisinnabhāvaṃ vata me satthā aññāsi, saṅghamajjhe dāni codanaṃ kāressāmī”ti dasabalassa santikaṃ gantvā vanditvā nisīdi. Bhagavā “kuhiṃ gatosi, sāriputtā”ti vatvā “na patirūpaṃ dāni te mayhaṃ anantare ṭhāne ṭhatvā vicarantassa asammajjitvā nisīditu”nti āha. Tato paṭṭhāya thero gaṇṭhikapaṭimuñcanaṭṭhānepi tiṭṭhanto pādena kacavaraṃ viyūhitvāva tiṭṭhati.

‘The Teacher’s instruction is done’—this duty of sweeping is praised by the Buddhas. Therefore, by doing it, one does the Teacher’s instruction. Here is the story: It is said that the Venerable Sāriputta went to the Himalayas and, without sweeping a certain mountain recess, entered the attainment of cessation and sat down. The Blessed One, reflecting, knew the elder’s state of sitting without sweeping and, traveling through the air, showed his footprints in the unswept spot before the elder and then returned. The elder, having emerged from his attainment, saw the Blessed One’s footprints and, establishing strong shame and moral dread, knelt down and thought, ‘Alas, the Teacher knew that I sat down without sweeping! Now I will bring about a censure in the midst of the Saṅgha.’ He went to the presence of the Ten-Powered One, paid homage, and sat down. The Blessed One said, ‘Where have you been, Sāriputta?’ and then added, ‘It is not proper for you now, who stands and wanders in a place so near to me, to sit down without sweeping.’ From then on, the elder, even when standing in a place for fastening the knot of his robe, would stand only after sweeping away the rubbish with his foot.

Attano bhāsapariyantaṃ na uggaṇhātīti “imasmiṃ vatthusmiṃ ettakaṃ suttaṃ upalabbhati, ettako vinicchayo, ettakaṃ suttañca vinicchayañca vakkhāmī”ti evaṃ attano bhāsapariyantaṃ na uggaṇhāti. “Ayaṃ codakassa purimakathā, ayaṃ pacchimakathā, ayaṃ cuditakassa purimakathā, ayaṃ pacchimakathā, ettakaṃ gayhūpagaṃ, ettakaṃ na gayhūpaga”nti evaṃ anuggaṇhanto pana parassa bhāsapariyantaṃ na uggaṇhāti nāma. Āpattiṃ na jānātīti pārājikaṃ vā saṅghādisesaṃ vāti sattannaṃ āpattikkhandhānaṃ nānākaraṇaṃ na jānāti. Mūlanti dve āpattiyā mūlāni kāyo ca vācā ca, tāni na jānāti. Samudayanti cha āpattisamuṭṭhānāni āpattisamudayo [P 7.1338] nāma, tāni na jānāti. Pārājikādīnaṃ vatthuṃ na jānātītipi vuttaṃ hoti. Nirodhanti ayaṃ āpatti desanāya nirujjhati, vūpasammati, ayaṃ vuṭṭhānenāti evaṃ āpattinirodhaṃ na jānāti. Satta samathe ajānanto pana āpattinirodhagāminipaṭipadaṃ na jānāti.

‘Not grasping the extent of one’s own speech’ means not grasping thus: ‘In this matter, this much of the text is found, this much is the decision; I will speak this much of the text and the decision.’ Furthermore, one who does not grasp thus: ‘This is the accuser’s initial statement, this is the later statement; this is the accused’s initial statement, this is the later statement; this much is to be accepted, this much is not to be accepted,’ is called one who does not grasp the extent of another’s speech. ‘Not knowing the offense’ means not knowing the distinctions among the seven groups of offenses, such as pārājika or saṅghādisesa. ‘The root’ means not knowing the two roots of an offense: body and speech. ‘The origin’ means not knowing the six origins of offenses, which are called the arising of an offense. It is also said that this means not knowing the basis of pārājika and other offenses. ‘The cessation’ means not knowing thus how an offense ceases: ‘This offense ceases by confession, it is allayed; this one ceases by rehabilitation.’ Furthermore, one who does not know the seven methods of settlement does not know the path leading to the cessation of offenses.

Adhikaraṇapañcake adhikaraṇaṃ nāma cattāri adhikaraṇāni. Adhikaraṇassa mūlaṃ nāma tettiṃsa mūlāni – vivādādhikaraṇassa dvādasa mūlāni, anuvādādhikaraṇassa cuddasa, āpattādhikaraṇassa cha, kiccādhikaraṇassa ekaṃ; tāni parato āvi bhavissanti. Adhikaraṇasamudayo nāma adhikaraṇasamuṭṭhānaṃ. Vivādādhikaraṇaṃ aṭṭhārasa bhedakaravatthūni nissāya uppajjati; anuvādādhikaraṇaṃ catasso vipattiyo; āpattādhikaraṇaṃ sattāpattikkhandhe; kiccādhikaraṇaṃ cattāri saṅghakiccānīti imaṃ vibhāgaṃ na jānātīti attho[M 0.0177]. Adhikaraṇanirodhaṃ na jānātīti dhammena vinayena satthusāsanena mūlāmūlaṃ gantvā vinicchayasamathaṃ pāpetuṃ na sakkoti[V 0.0177]; “idaṃ adhikaraṇaṃ dvīhi, idaṃ catūhi, idaṃ tīhi idaṃ ekena samathena sammatī”ti evaṃ satta samathe ajānanto pana adhikaraṇanirodhagāminipaṭipadaṃ na jānāti nāma. Vatthuṃ na jānātīti “idaṃ pārājikassa vatthu, idaṃ saṅghādisesassā”ti evaṃ sattannaṃ āpattikkhandhānaṃ vatthuṃ na jānāti. Nidānanti “sattannaṃ nidānānaṃ idaṃ sikkhāpadaṃ ettha paññattaṃ, idaṃ etthā”ti na jānāti. Paññattiṃ na jānātīti tasmiṃ tasmiṃ sikkhāpade paṭhamapaññattiṃ na jānāti. Anupaññattinti punappunaṃ paññattiṃ na jānāti. Anusandhivacanapathanti kathānusandhi-vinicchayānusandhivasena vatthuṃ na jānāti. Ñattiṃ na jānātīti sabbena sabbaṃ ñattiṃ na jānāti. Ñattiyā karaṇaṃ na jānātīti ñattikiccaṃ na jānāti, osāraṇādīsu navasu ṭhānesu ñattikammaṃ nāma hoti, ñattidutiyañatticatutthakammesu ñattiyā kammappatto hutvā tiṭṭhatīti na jānāti. Na pubbakusalo hoti na aparakusaloti pubbe kathetabbañca pacchā kathetabbañca na jānāti, ñatti nāma pubbe ṭhapetabbā, pacchā na ṭhapetabbātipi na jānāti. Akālaññū ca hotīti kālaṃ na jānāti, anajjhiṭṭho ayācito bhāsati, ñattikālampi ñattikhettampi [P 7.1339] ñattiokāsampi na jānāti.

In the Adhikaraṇapañcaka, ‘legal issue’ means the four legal issues. The ‘root’ of a legal issue means thirty-three roots: twelve roots for vivādādhikaraṇa (issues arising from disputes), fourteen for anuvādādhikaraṇa (issues arising from accusations), six for āpattādhikaraṇa (issues arising from offenses), and one for kiccādhikaraṇa (issues arising from formal acts); these will be made clear later. The ‘origin of a legal issue’ means the origination of the legal issue. Vivādādhikaraṇa arises dependent on eighteen grounds for creating divisions; anuvādādhikaraṇa on four kinds of failures; āpattādhikaraṇa on the seven groups of offenses; kiccādhikaraṇa on the four formal acts of the Saṅgha—the meaning is not knowing this division. ‘Not knowing the cessation of legal issues’ means being unable to go to the root of the matter by means of the Dhamma, the Vinaya, and the Teacher’s instruction, and to bring it to a settled decision. Furthermore, one who does not know the seven methods of settlement—such as ‘this legal issue is settled by two, this by four, this by three, this by one’—is called a person who does not know the path leading to the cessation of legal issues. ‘Not knowing the basis’ means not knowing, ‘This is the basis for a pārājika offense, this for a saṅghādisesa offense,’ and so on for the seven groups of offenses. ‘The origin’ means not knowing, ‘Among the seven origins, this training rule was laid down here, and this one here.’ ‘Not knowing the enactment’ means not knowing the first enactment in each training rule. ‘The supplementary enactment’ means not knowing the repeated enactment. ‘The path of connected speech’ means not knowing the matter by way of a connected discussion or a connected decision. ‘Not knowing the motion’ means not knowing the motion in every respect. ‘Not knowing the procedure of the motion’ means not knowing the function of the motion; not knowing that in nine instances, beginning with rehabilitation, there is what is called a motion-act; and not knowing that in motion-second and motion-fourth acts, once the motion is established, one has arrived at the formal act. ‘Not being skilled in what comes before or after’ means not knowing what should be spoken first and what later, and also not knowing that the motion should be established first and not afterward. ‘And being one who does not know the proper time’ means not knowing the time, speaking uninvited and unasked, and not knowing the time for the motion, the field for the motion, or the opportunity for the motion.

Mandattā momūhattāti kevalaṃ aññāṇena momūhabhāvena dhutaṅge ānisaṃsaṃ ajānitvā. Pāpicchoti tena araññavāsena paccayalābhaṃ patthayamāno. Pavivekanti kāyacittaupadhivivekaṃ. Idamatthitanti imāya kalyāṇāya paṭipattiyā attho etassāti idamatthi, idamatthino bhāvo idamatthitā; taṃ idamatthitaṃyeva nissāya na aññaṃ kiñci lokāmisanti attho.

‘Because of dullness, because of delusion’ means solely due to ignorance and a state of delusion, not knowing the benefit of the ascetic practice. ‘With evil desires’ means desiring the gain of requisites by means of that forest-dwelling. ‘Desiring solitude’ means desiring seclusion of body, mind, and the substrates of existence. ‘Being for this purpose’ means: for this person, there is a purpose in this noble practice, thus he is ‘one for whom this is the purpose’; the state of being one for whom this is the purpose is ‘being for this purpose’ (idamatthitā); the meaning is that one becomes a forest-dweller relying solely on that state of being for this purpose, and not on any other worldly allurement.

Uposathaṃ na jānātīti navavidhaṃ uposathaṃ na jānāti. Uposathakammanti adhammenavaggādibhedaṃ catubbidhaṃ uposathakammaṃ na jānāti. Pātimokkhanti dve mātikā na jānāti. Pātimokkhuddesanti sabbampi navavidhaṃ pātimokkhuddesaṃ na jānāti. Pavāraṇanti navavidhaṃ pavāraṇaṃ na jānāti. Pavāraṇākammaṃ uposathakammasadisameva.

'He does not know the Uposatha' means he does not know the nine kinds of Uposatha. 'He does not know the Uposatha-kamma' means he does not know the four kinds of Uposatha-kamma, which are differentiated as those performed by an unlawful group, and so on. 'He does not know the Pātimokkha' means he does not know the two matrices. 'He does not know the recitation of the Pātimokkha' means he does not know the entire nine kinds of recitation of the Pātimokkha. 'He does not know the Pavāraṇā' means he does not know the nine kinds of Pavāraṇā. The Pavāraṇā-kamma is just like the Uposatha-kamma.

Apāsādikapañcake [M 0.0178] – apāsādikanti kāyaduccaritādi akusalakammaṃ vuccati. Pāsādikanti kāyasucaritādi kusalakammaṃ vuccati. Ativelanti velaṃ atikkamma bahutaraṃ kālaṃ kulesu appaṃ vihāreti attho. Otāroti kilesānaṃ anto otaraṇaṃ. Saṃkiliṭṭhanti duṭṭhullāpattikāyasaṃsaggādibhedaṃ[V 0.0178]. Visuddhipañcakepavāraṇāggahaṇena navavidhāpi pavāraṇā veditabbā. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānamevāti.

In the pentad on the unpleasing: 'unpleasing' refers to unwholesome action such as bodily misconduct. 'Pleasing' refers to wholesome action such as good bodily conduct. 'Excessive' means: having exceeded the time limit, one dwells for a longer time among families and for a short time in the monastery. 'Opportunity' means the entering into the interior of the defilements. 'Defiled' refers to the differentiation into a grave offense, an offense of bodily contact, and so on. In the pentad on purity, by the taking of Pavāraṇā, the nine kinds of Pavāraṇā should also be known. The remainder everywhere is clear indeed.

Pañcakavāravaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The explanation of the section on pentads is finished.

Chakkavāravaṇṇanā

The Explanation of the Section on Hexads

326. Chakkesu – cha sāmīciyoti “so ca bhikkhu anabbhito, te ca bhikkhū gārayhā, ayaṃ tattha sāmīci”, “yuñjantāyasmanto sakaṃ, mā vo sakaṃ vinassāti ayaṃ tattha sāmīci”, “ayaṃ te bhikkhu patto yāva bhedanāya dhāretabboti ayaṃ tattha sāmīci”, “tato nīharitvā bhikkhūhi saddhiṃ saṃvibhajitabbaṃ, ayaṃ tattha sāmīci”, “aññātabbaṃ paripucchitabbaṃ paripañhitabbaṃ, ayaṃ tattha sāmīci”, “yassa bhavissati so harissatīti ayaṃ tattha sāmīcī”ti imā bhikkhupātimokkheyeva cha sāmīciyo. Cha chedanakāti pañcake vuttā pañca bhikkhunīnaṃ udakasāṭikāya saddhiṃ cha. Chahākārehīti alajjitā aññāṇatā kukkuccapakatatā akappiye kappiyasaññitā kappiye akappiyasaññitā satisammosāti. Tattha ekarattachārattasattāhātikkamādīsu āpattiṃ satisammosena [P 7.1340] āpajjati. Sesaṃ vuttanayameva. Cha ānisaṃsā vinayadhareti pañcake vuttā pañca tassādheyyo uposathoti iminā saddhiṃ cha.

326. In the hexads—six proprieties: 'That bhikkhu is not rehabilitated, and those bhikkhus are blameworthy—this is the propriety therein'; 'Venerable sirs, apply yourselves to your own task; let not your own task be lost—this is the propriety therein'; 'This bowl is for you, bhikkhu, to be kept until it breaks—this is the propriety therein'; 'Having taken it from there, it is to be shared with the bhikkhus—this is the propriety therein'; 'It should be known, it should be asked about, it should be investigated—this is the propriety therein'; 'He for whom it will be, he will take it—this is the propriety therein.' Thus, these six proprieties are in the Bhikkhu Pātimokkha itself. Six cutting-off offenses: the five mentioned in the pentad, together with the bhikkhunīs' bathing-cloth offense, make six. By six reasons: shamelessness, ignorance, being overcome by scrupulousness, perceiving the unallowable as allowable, perceiving the allowable as unallowable, and loss of mindfulness. Therein, in cases such as exceeding one night, six nights, or seven days, one falls into an offense through loss of mindfulness. The remainder is just as has been explained. Six benefits for a Vinaya-expert: the five mentioned in the pentad, together with this one, 'the Uposatha is to be undertaken by him,' make six.

Cha paramānīti “dasāhaparamaṃ atirekacīvaraṃ dhāretabbaṃ, māsaparamaṃ tena bhikkhunā taṃ cīvaraṃ nikkhipitabbaṃ, santaruttaraparamaṃ tena bhikkhunā tato cīvaraṃ sāditabbaṃ, chakkhattuparamaṃ tuṇhībhūtena uddissa ṭhātabbaṃ, navaṃ pana bhikkhunā santhataṃ kārāpetvā chabbassāni dhāretabbaṃ chabbassaparamatā dhāretabbaṃ, tiyojanaparamaṃ sahatthā dhāretabbāni, dasāhaparamaṃ atirekapatto dhāretabbo, sattāhaparamaṃ sannidhikārakaṃ paribhuñjitabbāni, chārattaparamaṃ tena bhikkhunā tena cīvarena vippavasitabbaṃ, catukkaṃsaparamaṃ, aḍḍhateyyakaṃsaparamaṃ, dvaṅgulapabbaparamaṃ ādātabbaṃ, aṭṭhaṅgulaparamaṃ mañcapaṭipādakaṃ[M 0.0179], aṭṭhaṅgulaparamaṃ dantakaṭṭha”nti imāni cuddasa paramāni. Tattha paṭhamāni cha ekaṃ chakkaṃ, tato ekaṃ apanetvā sesesu ekekaṃ pakkhipitvātiādinā nayena aññānipi chakkāni kātabbāni.

Six limits: 'An extra robe is to be kept for at most ten days; that robe is to be set aside by that bhikkhu for at most a month; a bhikkhu may accept a robe from that, at most enough for an under-robe and an upper-robe; one should stand silently, having aimed at a donor, for at most six times; having had a new felt rug made, a bhikkhu is to keep it for six years, it is to be kept for at most six years; they are to be carried by one's own hand for at most three yojanas; an extra bowl is to be kept for at most ten days; things stored up are to be consumed within at most seven days; that bhikkhu is to dwell apart from that robe for at most six nights; at most four kaṃsas; at most two and a half kaṃsas; it is to be taken at most two finger-joints; a bed-leg of at most eight finger-breadths; a tooth-stick of at most eight finger-breadths.' Thus, there are these fourteen limits. Therein, the first six are one hexad. Then, by removing one and adding one from the remainder, in this way, other hexads are also to be formed.

Cha [V 0.0179] āpattiyoti tīṇi chakkāni antarapeyyāle vuttāni. Cha kammānīti tajjanīya-niyassa-pabbājanīya-paṭisāraṇīyāni cattāri, āpattiyā adassane ca appaṭikamme ca vuttadvayampi ekaṃ, pāpikāya diṭṭhiyā appaṭinissagge ekanti cha. Nahāneti orenaḍḍhamāsaṃ nahāne; vippakatacīvarādichakkadvayaṃ kathinakkhandhake niddiṭṭhaṃ. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānamevāti.

Six offenses: three hexads have been spoken of in the intermediate peyyāla section. Six acts: the four acts of rebuke, subordination, banishment, and reconciliation; the one act for both not seeing an offense and not making amends for it; and the one act for not abandoning a bad view—thus, there are six acts. In bathing: this refers to the training rule on bathing within half a month; the two hexads beginning with 'the unfinished robe' are pointed out in the Kathinakkhandhaka. The remainder everywhere is clear indeed.

Chakkavāravaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The explanation of the section on hexads is finished.

Sattakavāravaṇṇanā

The Explanation of the Section on Heptads

327. Sattakesu – satta sāmīciyoti pubbe vuttesu chasu “sā ca bhikkhunī anabbhitā, tā ca bhikkhuniyo gārayhā, ayaṃ tattha sāmīcī”ti imaṃ pakkhipitvā satta veditabbā. Satta adhammikā paṭiññātakaraṇāti “bhikkhu pārājikaṃ ajjhāpanno hoti, pārājikena codiyamāno ‘saṅghādisesaṃ ajjhāpannomhī’ti paṭijānāti, taṃ saṅgho saṅghādisesena kāreti, adhammikaṃ paṭiññātakaraṇa”nti evaṃ samathakkhandhake niddiṭṭhā. Dhammikāpi tattheva niddiṭṭhā. Sattannaṃ anāpatti sattāhakaraṇīyena gantunti vassūpanāyikakkhandhake vuttaṃ[P 7.1341]. Sattānisaṃsā vinayadhareti “tassādheyyo uposatho pavāraṇā”ti imehi saddhiṃ pañcake vuttā pañca satta honti. Satta paramānīti chakke vuttāniyeva sattakavasena yojetabbāni. Katacīvarantiādīni dve sattakāni kathinakkhandhake niddiṭṭhāni.

327. In the heptads: As for the seven proprieties, they should be understood as seven by adding this one—'And that bhikkhunī is unrehabilitated, while those bhikkhunīs are censurable; this is the propriety there'—to the six previously mentioned. The seven unlawful acts based on acknowledgement are pointed out in the Samathakkhandhaka thus: 'A bhikkhu has committed a pārājika offense. Being charged with a pārājika, he acknowledges, "I have committed a saṅghādisesa offense." The Saṅgha then deals with him for a saṅghādisesa. This is an unlawful act based on acknowledgement.' The lawful ones are also pointed out in that same place. The heptad concerning 'for seven types of persons, there is no offense in going on a journey with a seven-day allowance' is stated in the Vassūpanāyikakkhandhaka. As for the seven benefits for a Vinaya-expert, the five mentioned in the pentad become seven when combined with these: 'The Uposatha and Pavāraṇā are to be undertaken by him.' As for the seven limits, the very ones mentioned in the hexad should be applied by way of a heptad. The two heptads beginning with 'the robe-making is finished' are pointed out in the Kathinakkhandhaka.

Bhikkhussa na hoti āpatti daṭṭhabbā, bhikkhussa hoti āpatti daṭṭhabbā, bhikkhussa hoti āpatti paṭikātabbāti imāni tīṇi sattakāni, dve adhammikāni, ekaṃ dhammikaṃ; tāni tīṇipi campeyyake niddiṭṭhāni. Asaddhammāti asataṃ dhammā, asanto vā dhammā; asobhanā hīnā lāmakāti attho. Saddhammāti sataṃ buddhādīnaṃ dhammā; santo vā dhammā sundarā uttamāti attho. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānamevāti.

'For a bhikkhu, there is no offense to be seen'; 'For a bhikkhu, there is an offense to be seen'; 'For a bhikkhu, there is an offense to be remedied'—these are three heptads. Two are unlawful, one is lawful; all three are pointed out in the Campeyyakkhandhaka. 'Untrue Dhamma' means the principles of the unvirtuous, or unwholesome principles; the meaning is that they are unseemly, inferior, and base. 'True Dhamma' means the principles of the virtuous, such as the Buddha and others, or wholesome principles; the meaning is that they are beautiful and excellent. The remainder in all cases is self-evident.

Sattakavāravaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The explanation of the section on heptads is finished.

Aṭṭhakavāravaṇṇanā

The Explanation of the Section on Octads

328. Aṭṭhakesu [V 0.0180][M 0.0180] – aṭṭhānisaṃseti “na mayaṃ iminā bhikkhunā saddhiṃ uposathaṃ karissāma, vinā iminā bhikkhunā uposathaṃ karissāma, na mayaṃ iminā bhikkhunā saddhiṃ pavāressāma, saṅghakammaṃ karissāma, āsane nisīdissāma, yāgupāne nisīdissāma, bhattagge nisīdissāma, ekacchanne vasissāma, yathāvuḍḍhaṃ abhivādanaṃ paccuṭṭhānaṃ añjalikammaṃ sāmīcikammaṃ karissāma, vinā iminā bhikkhunā karissāmā”ti evaṃ kosambakakkhandhake vutte ānisaṃse. Dutiyaaṭṭhakepi eseva nayo, tampi hi evameva kosambakakkhandhake vuttaṃ.

328. In the sections on eight: As for the eight benefits, these are the benefits stated in the Kosambakakkhandhaka thus: ‘We will not perform the Uposatha with this bhikkhu... (and so on for Pavāraṇā, formal acts of the Saṅgha, sitting on seats, at the gruel-distribution, in the refectory, and living under one roof)... we will perform the paying of respects, rising, making the añjali gesture, and proper duties according to seniority; we will do these things without this bhikkhu.’ For the second eightfold group, this is the same method, for that too is stated in the Kosambakakkhandhaka in this very way.

Aṭṭha yāvatatiyakāti bhikkhūnaṃ terasake cattāro, bhikkhunīnaṃ sattarasake bhikkhūhi asādhāraṇā cattāroti aṭṭha. Aṭṭhahākārehi kulāni dūsetīti kulāni dūseti pupphena vā phalena vā cuṇṇena vā mattikāya vā dantakaṭṭhena vā veḷuyā vā vejjikāya vā jaṅghapesanikena vāti imehi aṭṭhahi. Aṭṭha mātikā cīvarakkhandhake, aparā aṭṭha kathinakkhandhake vuttā. Aṭṭhahi asaddhammehīti lābhena alābhena yasena ayasena sakkārena asakkārena pāpicchatāya pāpamittatāya. Aṭṭha lokadhammā nāma lābhe sārāgo, alābhe paṭivirodho; evaṃ yase ayase, pasaṃsāya nindāya, sukhe sārāgo, dukkhe paṭivirodhoti. Aṭṭhaṅgiko musāvādoti “vinidhāya sañña”nti [P 7.1342] iminā saddhiṃ pāḷiyaṃ āgatehi sattahīti aṭṭhahi aṅgehi aṭṭhaṅgiko.

As for ‘eight offenses requiring admonition up to the third time’: four for bhikkhus among the thirteen Saṅghādisesas, and four for bhikkhunīs among the seventeen Saṅghādisesas that are not shared with bhikkhus—thus there are eight. As for ‘he corrupts families by eight means’: he corrupts families by these eight—with flowers, or fruit, or powder, or clay, or a tooth-stick, or bamboo, or by practicing medicine, or by acting as a messenger on foot. Eight mātikās are stated in the Cīvarakkhandhaka, and another eight in the Kathinakkhandhaka. As for ‘by eight untrue dhammas’: by gain, loss, status, lack of status, honor, dishonor, evil wishes, and evil friendship. The eight worldly conditions are named thus: strong passion for gain, aversion to loss; similarly for status and lack of status, for praise and blame, strong passion for pleasure, and aversion to pain. As for ‘eight-factored false speech’: together with this factor, ‘altering one’s perception,’ and the seven factors that come in the Pāḷi text, false speech becomes eight-factored.

Aṭṭha uposathaṅgānīti –

As for the eight Uposatha factors—

“Pāṇaṃ na hane na cādinnamādiye,

Musā na bhāse na ca majjapo siyā;

Abrahmacariyā virameyya methunā,

Rattiṃ na bhuñjeyya vikālabhojanaṃ.

“One should not kill a living being, nor take what is not given; one should not speak falsely, nor be a drinker of intoxicants; one should abstain from unchastity, from sexual intercourse; one should not eat at night, at the wrong time.

“Mālaṃ na dhāre na ca gandhamācare,

Mañce chamāyaṃva sayetha santhate;

Etañhi [V 0.0181] aṭṭhaṅgikamāhuposathaṃ,

Buddhena dukkhantagunā pakāsita”nti. (a. ni. 3.71);

“One should not wear garlands nor apply perfumes; one should sleep on a mat on the ground. For this is the eight-factored Uposatha, declared by the Buddha who has gone to the end of suffering.”

Evaṃ [M 0.0181] vuttāni aṭṭha. Aṭṭha dūteyyaṅgānīti “idha, bhikkhave, bhikkhu sotā ca hoti sāvetā cā”tiādinā nayena saṅghabhedake vuttāni. Titthiyavattāni mahākhandhake niddiṭṭhāni. Anatirittā ca atirittā ca pavāraṇāsikkhāpade niddiṭṭhā. Aṭṭhannaṃ paccuṭṭhātabbanti bhattagge vuḍḍhabhikkhunīnaṃ, āsanampi tāsaṃyeva dātabbaṃ. Upāsikāti visākhā. Aṭṭhānisaṃsā vinayadhareti pañcake vuttesu pañcasu “tassādheyyo uposatho, pavāraṇā, saṅghakamma”nti ime tayo pakkhipitvā aṭṭha veditabbā. Aṭṭha paramānīti pubbe vuttaparamāneva aṭṭhakavasena yojetvā veditabbāni. Aṭṭhasu dhammesu sammā vattitabbanti “na pakatattassa bhikkhuno uposatho ṭhapetabbo, na pavāraṇā ṭhapetabbā”tiādinā nayena samathakkhandhake niddiṭṭhesu aṭṭhasu. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānamevāti.

Thus were stated the eight Uposatha factors. The eight factors of an envoy are stated in the Saṅghabhedakakkhandhaka in the manner of ‘Here, bhikkhus, a bhikkhu is both a listener and a relater...’ The observances of sectarians are pointed out in the Mahākhandhaka. The rules regarding what is not left over and what is left over are pointed out in the Pavāraṇā training rule. As for the eight for whom one should rise: in the refectory, one should rise for eight senior bhikkhunīs, and a seat should also be given to them. The female lay follower is Visākhā. The eight benefits for a Vinaya-expert: by adding these three—‘The Uposatha, Pavāraṇā, and a formal act of the Saṅgha are dependent on him’—to the five stated in the section on fives, they should be understood as eight. The eight supreme things: the previously mentioned supreme things should be understood by applying them by way of an eightfold grouping. One should act rightly in eight matters: in the eight matters pointed out in the Samathakkhandhaka in the manner of ‘The Uposatha should not be suspended for a bhikkhu of normal status, nor should the Pavāraṇā be suspended...’ The remainder in all cases is self-evident.

Aṭṭhakavāravaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Section on Eights is finished.

Navakavāravaṇṇanā

The Commentary on the Section on Nines

329. Navakesu – nava āghātavatthūnīti “anatthaṃ me acarī”tiādīni nava. Nava āghātapaṭivinayāti “anatthaṃ me acari, taṃ kutettha labbhāti āghātaṃ paṭivinetī”tiādīni nava. Nava vinītavatthūnīti navahi āghātavatthūhi ārati virati paṭivirati setughāto. Navahi saṅgho bhijjatīti “navannaṃ vā, upāli, atirekanavannaṃ vā saṅgharāji ceva hoti saṅghabhedo cā”ti. Nava paramānīti pubbe vuttaparamāneva navakavasena yojetvā veditabbāni. Nava taṇhāmūlakā nāma taṇhaṃ paṭicca pariyesanā, pariyesanaṃ paṭicca lābho, lābhaṃ paṭicca vinicchayo, vinicchayaṃ paṭicca chandarāgo, chandarāgaṃ [P 7.1343] paṭicca ajjhosānaṃ, ajjhosānaṃ paṭicca pariggaho, pariggahaṃ paṭicca macchariyaṃ, macchariyaṃ paṭicca ārakkhā, ārakkhādhikaraṇaṃ daṇḍādānasatthādānakalahaviggahavivādatuvaṃtuvaṃpesuññamusāvādā. Nava vidhamānāti “seyyassa seyyohamasmī”timānādayo. Nava cīvarānīti ticīvaranti vā vassikasāṭikāti vātiādinā nayena vuttāni. Na vikappetabbānīti adhiṭṭhitakālato paṭṭhāya na vikappetabbāni[V 0.0182]. Nava adhammikāni dānānīti saṅghassa pariṇataṃ aññasaṅghassa vā cetiyassa vā puggalassa vā pariṇāmeti, cetiyassa pariṇataṃ aññacetiyassa vā saṅghassa vā puggalassa vā pariṇāmeti[M 0.0182], puggalassa pariṇataṃ aññapuggalassa vā saṅghassa vā cetiyassa vā pariṇāmetīti evaṃ vuttāni.

329. In the section on nines—the nine grounds for resentment are: ‘He has done me harm,’ and so on. The nine ways of dispelling resentment are: ‘He has done me harm; how could it be obtained here that this were not so?’ and so on. The nine disciplined states are: from the nine grounds for resentment, there is abstention, refraining, desisting, and the destruction of the causeway. The Saṅgha is split by nine things: ‘Upāli, on account of nine or more than nine, there arises both a fissure in the Saṅgha and a schism in the Saṅgha.’ The nine supreme things should be understood by applying the previously mentioned supreme things in a ninefold manner. The nine things rooted in craving are: dependent on craving, there is seeking; dependent on seeking, there is gain; dependent on gain, there is decision; dependent on decision, there is desire and lust; dependent on desire and lust, there is attachment; dependent on attachment, there is possessiveness; dependent on possessiveness, there is stinginess; dependent on stinginess, there is guarding; and with guarding as the cause, there is the taking up of sticks and weapons, quarrels, disputes, arguments, accusations, slander, and false speech. The nine kinds of conceit are: conceits such as, ‘I am better than one who is better.’ The nine robes are those stated in the manner of ‘the three robes,’ or ‘the rains-cloth,’ and so on. They should not be disposed of by sharing: from the time they are determined, they should not be disposed of by sharing. The nine unrighteous gifts are those stated thus: one diverts what is designated for the Saṅgha to another Saṅgha, or to a shrine, or to an individual; one diverts what is designated for a shrine to another shrine, or to the Saṅgha, or to an individual; one diverts what is designated for an individual to another individual, or to the Saṅgha, or to a shrine.

Nava paṭiggahaparibhogāti etesaṃyeva dānānaṃ paṭiggahā ca paribhogā ca. Tīṇi dhammikāni dānānīti saṅghassa ninnaṃ saṅghasseva deti, cetiyassa ninnaṃ cetiyasseva, puggalassa ninnaṃ puggalasseva detīti imāni tīṇi. Paṭiggahapaṭibhogāpi tesaṃyeva paṭiggahā ca paribhogā ca. Nava adhammikā saññattiyoti adhammavādipuggalo, adhammavādisambahulā, adhammavādisaṅghoti evaṃ tīṇi tikāni samathakkhandhake niddiṭṭhāni. Dhammikā saññattiyopi dhammavādī puggalotiādinā nayena tattheva niddiṭṭhā. Adhammakamme dve navakāni ovādavaggassa paṭhamasikkhāpadaniddese pācittiyavasena vuttāni. Dhammakamme dve navakāni tattheva dukkaṭavasena vuttāni. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānamevāti.

Regarding the nine kinds of accepting and using: these are the accepting and using of these very gifts. The three righteous gifts are: one gives what is designated for the Sangha to the Sangha only, what is designated for a shrine to the shrine only, and what is designated for an individual to the individual only—these are the three. Accepting and using are also the accepting and using of these very gifts. The nine unrighteous declarations are: an individual who speaks unrighteously, a group who speaks unrighteously, and a Sangha that speaks unrighteously—thus these three triads are explained in the Samathakkhandhaka. Righteous declarations are also explained there in the same way, beginning with 'an individual who speaks righteously.' Two sets of nine in unrighteous formal acts are stated in the explanation of the first training rule in the Ovāda chapter under the Pācittiya offenses. Two sets of nine in righteous formal acts are stated there under the Dukkaṭa offenses. The rest is clear throughout.

Navakavāravaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Section of Nines is completed.

Dasakavāravaṇṇanā

Commentary on the Section of Tens

330. Dasakesu – dasa āghātavatthūnīti navakesu vuttāni nava “aṭṭhāne vā pana āghāto jāyatī”ti iminā saddhiṃ dasa honti. Āghātapaṭivinayāpi tattha vuttā nava “aṭṭhāne vā pana āghāto jāyati, taṃ kutettha labbhāti āghātaṃ paṭivinetī”ti iminā saddhiṃ dasa veditabbā. Dasa vinītavatthūnīti dasahi āghātavatthūhi viratisaṅkhātāni dasa. Dasavatthukā micchādiṭṭhīti “natthi dinna”ntiādivasena veditabbā, “atthi dinna”ntiādivasena sammādiṭṭhi, “sassato loko”tiādinā vasena pana antaggāhikā diṭṭhi veditabbā. Dasa micchattāti micchādiṭṭhiādayo [P 7.1344] micchāvimuttipariyosānā, viparītā sammattā. Salākaggāhā samathakkhandhake niddiṭṭhā.

330. In the Section of Tens: As for the ten bases for resentment, the nine spoken of in the Section of Nines, together with this one, “or resentment arises in an unreasonable place,” become ten. The removals of resentment are also to be known as ten: the nine spoken of there, together with this one, “or resentment arises in an unreasonable place. How is that to be got here? One should remove the resentment.” As for the ten disciplined grounds, they are the ten reckoned as abstention from the ten bases for resentment. As for the wrong view with ten bases, it is to be known by way of “there is nothing given,” and so on. Right view is to be known by way of “there is something given,” and so on. However, the view that grasps at extremes is to be known by way of “the world is eternal,” and so on. As for the ten wrongnesses, they are wrong view and so on, ending with wrong liberation; their opposites are the rightnesses. The takings of lots have been pointed out in the Chapter on Settling Legal Questions.

Dasahaṅgehi samannāgato bhikkhu ubbāhikāya sammannitabboti “sīlavā hotī”tiādinā nayena [V 0.0183] samathakkhandhake vuttehi dasahi. Dasa ādīnavā rājantepurappavesane rājasikkhāpade niddiṭṭhā. Dasa dānavatthūnīti annaṃ pānaṃ vatthaṃ yānaṃ mālā gandhaṃ vilepanaṃ seyyāvasathaṃ padīpeyyaṃ. Dasa ratanānīti muttāmaṇiveḷuriyādīni. Dasa paṃsukūlānīti sosānikaṃ, pāpaṇikaṃ[M 0.0183], undūrakkhāyitaṃ, upacikakkhāyitaṃ, aggidaḍḍhaṃ, gokhāyitaṃ, ajikakkhāyitaṃ, thūpacīvaraṃ, ābhisekiyaṃ, bhatapaṭiyābhatanti etesu upasampannena ussukkaṃ kātabbaṃ. Dasa cīvaradhāraṇāti “sabbanīlakāni cīvarāni dhārentī”ti vuttavasena dasāti kurundiyaṃ vuttaṃ. Mahāaṭṭhakathāyaṃ pana “navasu kappiyacīvaresu udakasāṭikaṃ vā saṅkaccikaṃ vā pakkhipitvā dasā”ti vuttaṃ.

A monk endowed with ten qualities is fit to be appointed by the act of authorization—with the ten spoken of in the Chapter on Settling Legal Questions by the method beginning, 'He is virtuous.' The ten dangers in entering the king's inner palace have been pointed out in the training rule concerning kings. The ten gift-items are: food, drink, cloth, a vehicle, garlands, perfume, unguent, lodging, and provision for lamps. The ten jewels are: pearls, gems, beryl, and so on. The ten types of rags-from-the-dust-heap are: from a charnel ground, from a shop, gnawed by rats, gnawed by termites, burnt by fire, gnawed by cattle, gnawed by goats, cloth from an anthill, from a consecration site, and cloth brought to and from the charnel ground. Regarding these, an ordained one should make an effort. As for the ten ways of wearing robes, it is said in the Kurundī commentary that there are ten by way of what was said: 'They wear robes that are entirely dark blue.' However, in the Mahā-aṭṭhakathā it is said: 'By including either a bathing-cloth or a chest-covering among the nine allowable robes, they become ten.'

Avandanīyapuggalā senāsanakkhandhake niddiṭṭhā. Dasa akkosavatthūni omasavāde niddiṭṭhāni. Dasa ākārā pesuññasikkhāpade niddiṭṭhā. Dasa senāsanānīti mañco, pīṭhaṃ, bhisi, bimbohanaṃ, cimilikā, uttarattharaṇaṃ, taṭṭikā, cammakhaṇḍo, nisīdanaṃ, tiṇasanthāro, paṇṇasanthāroti. Dasa varāni yāciṃsūti visākhā aṭṭha, suddhodanamahārājā ekaṃ, jīvako ekaṃ. Yāguānisaṃsā ca akappiyamaṃsāni ca bhesajjakkhandhake niddiṭṭhāni. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānamevāti.

The persons unworthy of veneration have been pointed out in the Chapter on Lodgings. The ten grounds for reviling have been pointed out in the training rule on contemptuous speech. The ten modes have been pointed out in the training rule on slander. The ten lodgings are: a couch, a chair, a stuffed mattress, a pillow, a coarse cloth, an upper covering, a wicker mat, a piece of hide, a sitting-cloth, a grass spread, and a leaf spread. As for ‘they asked for ten boons’: Visākhā asked for eight, Mahārāja Suddhodana for one, and Jīvaka for one. The benefits of rice-gruel and the unallowable meats have been pointed out in the Chapter on Medicines. The remainder in all cases is self-evident.

Dasakavāravaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Section of Tens is concluded.

Ekādasakavāravaṇṇanā

The Explanation of the Section on Elevens

331. Ekādasakesu – ekādasāti paṇḍakādayo ekādasa. Ekādasa pādukāti dasa ratanamayā, ekā kaṭṭhapādukā. Tiṇapādukamuñjapādukapabbajapādukādayo pana kaṭṭhapādukasaṅgahameva gacchanti. Ekādasa pattāti tambalohamayena vā dārumayena vā saddhiṃ dasa ratanamayā. Ekādasa cīvarānīti sabbanīlakādīni ekādasa. Yāvatatiyakāti ukkhittānuvattikā bhikkhunī, saṅghādisesā aṭṭha, ariṭṭho, caṇḍakāḷīti. Ekādasa antarāyikā nāma “nasi animittā”ti ādayo. Ekādasa [P 7.1345] cīvarāni adhiṭṭhātabbānīti ticīvaraṃ, vassikasāṭikā, nisīdanaṃ, paccattharaṇaṃ, kaṇḍuppaṭicchādi, mukhapuñchanacoḷaṃ, parikkhāracoḷaṃ, udakasāṭikā, saṅkaccikāti. Na vikappetabbānīti etāneva adhiṭṭhitakālato paṭṭhāya na vikappetabbāni. Gaṇṭhikā [V 0.0184] ca vidhā ca suttamayena saddhiṃ ekādasa honti, te sabbe khuddakakkhandhake niddiṭṭhā. Pathaviyo pathavisikkhāpade niddiṭṭhā. Nissayapaṭipassaddhiyo upajjhāyamhā pañca, ācariyamhā cha; evaṃ ekādasa. Avandiyapuggalā naggena saddhiṃ ekādasa, te sabbe senāsanakkhandhake niddiṭṭhā[M 0.0184]. Ekādasa paramāni pubbe vuttesu cuddasasu ekādasakavasena yojetvā veditabbāni. Ekādasa varānīti mahāpajāpatiyā yācitavarena saddhiṃ pubbe vuttāni dasa. Ekādasa sīmādosāti “atikhuddakaṃ sīmaṃ sammannantī”tiādinā nayena kammavagge āgamissanti.

331. In the groups of eleven—'eleven' refers to the eleven persons beginning with the eunuch. 'Eleven sandals' means ten made of jewels and one made of wood. Sandals made of grass, muñja grass, pabbaja grass, and so forth, are included under wooden sandals. 'Eleven bowls' means ten made of jewels, together with one made of copper-bronze or wood. 'Eleven robes' refers to the eleven beginning with the all-blue robe. 'Up to the third time' refers to the nun who follows a suspended bhikkhu, the eight Saṅghādisesa offenses, the bhikkhu Ariṭṭha, and the bhikkhunī Caṇḍakāḷī. The eleven impediments are those beginning with 'nasi animittā'. The eleven robes to be determined are: the set of three robes, the rainy-season robe, the sitting cloth, the covering cloth, the sore-covering cloth, the face-wiping cloth, the requisites-cloth, the water-bathing cloth, and the chest-band. 'Should not be alienated' means that these very robes, from the time they have been determined, should not be alienated. Knots and loops, together with those made of thread, are eleven; all these are explained in the Khuddakakkhandhaka. The types of earth are explained in the training rule on digging the earth. The cessations of dependence are five from a preceptor and six from a teacher; thus, there are eleven. The persons not to be venerated, together with a naked one, are eleven; all these are explained in the Senāsanakkhandhaka. The eleven supreme things should be understood by applying them, by way of the group of eleven, to the fourteen supreme things spoken of previously. The eleven boons are the ten spoken of previously, together with the boon requested by Mahāpajāpatī. The eleven faults of a boundary are those that will come in the Kammavagga by the method beginning with, 'they establish a boundary that is too small.'

Akkosakaparibhāsake puggale ekādasādīnavā nāma “yo so, bhikkhave, bhikkhu akkosako paribhāsako ariyūpavādī, sabrahmacārīnaṃ aṭṭhānametaṃ anavakāso yaṃ so ekādasannaṃ byasanānaṃ aññataraṃ byasanaṃ na nigaccheyya. Katamesaṃ ekādasannaṃ? Anadhigataṃ nādhigacchati, adhigatā parihāyati, saddhammassa na vodāyanti, saddhammesu vā adhimāniko hoti, anabhirato vā brahmacariyaṃ carati, aññataraṃ vā saṃkiliṭṭhaṃ āpattiṃ āpajjati, sikkhaṃ vā paccakkhāya hīnāyāvattati, gāḷhaṃ vā rogātaṅkaṃ phusati, ummādaṃ vā pāpuṇāti cittakkhepaṃ vā, sammūḷho kālaṃ karoti, kāyassa bhedā parammaraṇā apāyaṃ duggatiṃ vinipātaṃ nirayaṃ upapajjatī”ti (a. ni. 11.6). Ettha ca saddhammoti buddhavacanaṃ adhippetaṃ.

Regarding a person who insults and threatens, there are eleven dangers, namely: 'Bhikkhus, as to that bhikkhu who is insulting, threatening, and an accuser of noble ones among his companions in the holy life, it is impossible, it cannot be, that he would not meet with one of eleven misfortunes. Which eleven? He does not attain what has not been attained; he falls away from what has been attained; the good Dhammas do not become pure for him; or he becomes conceited regarding the good Dhammas; or he practices the holy life without delight; or he commits some defiled offense; or having rejected the training, he reverts to the lower life; or he is afflicted with a severe illness; or he attains madness or mental distraction; he dies deeply confused; after the breakup of the body, after death, he is reborn in a state of loss, a bad destination, a place of downfall, in hell.' And here, by 'good Dhamma,' the word of the Buddha is intended.

Āsevitāyāti ādito paṭṭhāya sevitāya. Bhāvitāyāti nipphāditāya vaḍḍhitāya

'Practiced' means practiced from the beginning. 'Developed' means brought to completion or increased.

Vā. Bahulīkatāyāti punappunaṃ katāya. Yānīkatāyāti suyuttayānasadisāya katāya. Vatthukatāyāti yathā patiṭṭhā hoti; evaṃ katāya. Anuṭṭhitāyāti anu anu pavattitāya; niccādhiṭṭhitāyāti attho. Paricitāyāti samantato citāya; sabbadisāsu citāya ācitāya bhāvitāya abhivaḍḍhitāyāti attho. Susamāraddhāyāti suṭṭhu samāraddhāya; vasībhāvaṃ upanītāyāti attho. Na pāpakaṃ supinanti pāpakameva na passati, bhadrakaṃ pana vuḍḍhikāraṇabhūtaṃ [P 7.1346] passati. Devatā rakkhantīti ārakkhadevatā dhammikaṃ rakkhaṃ paccupaṭṭhāpenti. Tuvaṭaṃ cittaṃ samādhiyatīti khippaṃ cittaṃ samādhiyati. Uttari appaṭivijjhantoti mettājhānato uttariṃ arahattaṃ asacchikaronto sekho vā puthujjano vā hutvā kālaṃ karonto brahmalokūpago hoti. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānamevāti.

'Made much of' means done again and again. 'Made a vehicle' means made like a well-yoked vehicle. 'Made a foundation' means done in such a way that it becomes a foundation; thus, done so as to be firmly established. 'Undertaken' means made to occur again and again; the meaning is 'constantly determined.' 'Well-acquainted' means accumulated from all around; the meaning is accumulated in all directions, accumulated from the start, developed, and further developed. 'Well-begun' means thoroughly undertaken; the meaning is 'brought to a state of mastery.' 'Does not see a bad dream' means one does not see a bad dream at all; rather, one sees a good dream that is a cause for growth. 'Deities protect' means guardian deities provide righteous protection. 'The mind is swiftly concentrated' means the mind quickly becomes concentrated. 'Without penetrating further' means that, without realizing Arahantship, which is higher than the mettā-jhāna, one who dies as either a trainee or an ordinary person is reborn in the Brahma-world. The rest is clear everywhere.

Ekādasakavāravaṇṇanā pariyosānā

The End of the Explanation of the Section on Elevens.

Ekuttarikavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Ekuttarika is finished.

Uposathādipucchāvissajjanā

Questions and Answers on the Uposatha, and so forth

332. “Uposathakammassa [M 0.0185][V 0.0185] ko ādī”tiādīnaṃ pucchānaṃ vissajjane sāmaggī ādīti “uposathaṃ karissāmā”ti sīmaṃ sodhetvā chandapārisuddhiṃ āharitvā sannipatitānaṃ kāyasāmaggī ādi. Kiriyā majjheti pubbakiccaṃ katvā pātimokkhaosāraṇakiriyā majjhe. Niṭṭhānaṃ pariyosānanti “tattha sabbeheva samaggehi sammodamānehi avivadamānehi sikkhitabba”nti idaṃ pātimokkhaniṭṭhānaṃ pariyosānaṃ. Pavāraṇākammassa sāmaggī ādīti “pavāraṇaṃ karissāmā”ti sīmaṃ sodhetvā chandapavāraṇaṃ āharitvā sannipatitānaṃ kāyasāmaggī ādi. Kiriyā majjheti pavāraṇāñatti ca pavāraṇākathā ca majjhe, saṅghanavakassa “passanto paṭikarissāmī”ti vacanaṃ pariyosānaṃ. Tajjanīyakammādīsu vatthu nāma yena vatthunā kammāraho hoti, taṃ vatthu. Puggaloti yena taṃ vatthu kataṃ, so puggalo. Kammavācā pariyosānanti “kataṃ saṅghena itthannāmassa bhikkhuno tajjanīyakammaṃ, khamati saṅghassa, tasmā tuṇhī, evametaṃ dhārayāmī”ti evaṃ tassā tassā kammavācāya avasānavacanaṃ pariyosānaṃ. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānamevāti

332. In the answer to questions such as, 'What is the beginning of the Uposatha act?': Harmony is the beginning. That is, after cleaning the boundary and having brought the purity of consent, the bodily harmony of those who have assembled thinking, 'We will perform the Uposatha,' is the beginning. The action is the middle. That is, after performing the preliminary duties, the act of reciting the Pātimokkha is the middle. The completion is the end. That is, this completion of the Pātimokkha, 'Therein, it should be trained in by all, being harmonious, rejoicing together, and not disputing,' is the end. For the Pavāraṇā act, harmony is the beginning. That is, after cleaning the boundary and having brought the consent for Pavāraṇā, the bodily harmony of those who have assembled thinking, 'We will perform the Pavāraṇā,' is the beginning. The action is the middle. That is, the motion for Pavāraṇā and the statement of Pavāraṇā are the middle; the statement of the most junior monk in the Sangha, 'If I see, I will make amends,' is the end. In the act of censure and so forth, the 'matter' is that matter on account of which one is worthy of the act. The 'person' is he by whom that matter was done. The formal act statement is the end. That is, the concluding words of each respective formal act statement, 'The act of censure has been carried out by the Sangha on the monk of such-and-such a name. It is approved by the Sangha; therefore, there is silence. Thus do I hold this,' is the end. The remainder is clear everywhere.

Uposathādipucchāvissajjanāvaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The commentary on the Questions and Answers concerning the Uposatha and so forth is concluded.

Atthavasapakaraṇāvaṇṇanā

Commentary on the Exposition on the Grounds for Benefits

334. Atthavasapakaraṇe – dasa atthavasetiādīsu yaṃ vattabbaṃ taṃ paṭhamapārājikavaṇṇanāyameva vuttaṃ. Yaṃ saṅghasuṭṭhu taṃ saṅghaphāsūtiādīsu uparimaṃ uparimaṃ padaṃ heṭṭhimassa heṭṭhimassa padassa attho.

334. In the Exposition on the Grounds for Benefits—regarding the text beginning 'ten grounds for benefits'—whatever should be said has already been stated in the commentary on the first Pārājika. In texts such as 'What is for the excellence of the Sangha, that is for the comfort of the Sangha,' each subsequent term is the meaning of the preceding term.

Atthasataṃ [V 0.0186] dhammasatantiādimhi pana yadetaṃ dasasu padesu ekekaṃ mūlaṃ katvā dasakkhattuṃ yojanāya padasataṃ [P 7.1347] vuttaṃ. Tattha pacchimassa pacchimassa padassa vasena atthasataṃ purimassa purimassa vasena dhammasataṃ veditabbaṃ. Atha vā ye dasa atthavase paṭicca tathāgatena sāvakānaṃ sikkhāpadaṃ paññattaṃ, ye pubbe paṭhamapārājikavaṇṇanāyaṃ “tattha saṅghasuṭṭhutā nāma saṅghassa suṭṭhubhāvo ‘suṭṭhu devā’ti āgataṭṭhāne viya ‘suṭṭhu bhante’ti vacanasampaṭicchanabhāvo, yo ca tathāgatassa vacanaṃ sampaṭicchati, tassa taṃ dīgharattaṃ [M 0.0186] hitāya sukhāya hoti, tasmā saṅghassa ‘suṭṭhu bhante’ti mama vacanasampaṭicchanatthaṃ paññapessāmi asampaṭicchane ādīnavaṃ sampaṭicchane ca ānisaṃsaṃ dassetvā na balakkārena abhibhavitvāti etamatthaṃ āvikaronto āha – saṅghasuṭṭhutāyā”ti evamādinā nayena vaṇṇitā, tesaṃ idha dasakkhattuṃ āgatattā atthasataṃ tadatthajotakānañca padānaṃ vasena dhammasataṃ veditabbaṃ. Idāni atthajotakānaṃ niruttīnaṃ vasena niruttisataṃ, dhammabhūtānaṃ niruttīnaṃ vasena niruttisatanti dve niruttisatāni, atthasate ñāṇasataṃ, dhammasate ñāṇasataṃ, dvīsu niruttisatesu dve ñāṇasatānīti cattāri ñāṇasatāni ca veditabbāni.

However, in the text beginning 'a hundred meanings, a hundred teachings,' this hundred terms has been stated by way of combining ten times, having made each one of the ten terms a root. Therein, a hundred meanings should be known by way of the latter term, and a hundred teachings by way of the former term. Or alternatively, there are the ten grounds for benefits on account of which the Tathāgata laid down the training rule for the disciples. These grounds for benefits were explained before in the commentary on the first Pārājika in the manner beginning: 'Herein, “excellence of the Sangha” means the Sangha's state of being excellent. Just as in the place where “Well, O gods!” occurs, so too is the state of accepting the words “Well, venerable sir!” And whoever accepts the Tathāgata's word, for him that is for long-term welfare and happiness. Therefore, for the Sangha, I will lay down a training rule for the acceptance of my word as “Well, venerable sir!”—showing the danger in non-acceptance and the benefit in acceptance, not overpowering them by force. Thus revealing this meaning, he said: “for the excellence of the Sangha.”' Since those grounds for benefits have occurred here ten times, a hundred meanings should be known, and by way of the terms that illuminate that meaning, a hundred teachings should be known. Now, by way of the expressions that illuminate the meaning, there is a hundred expressions; and by way of the expressions that have become the Dhamma, there is a hundred expressions—thus, two hundred expressions. And a hundred knowledges in the hundred meanings, a hundred knowledges in the hundred teachings, and two hundred knowledges in the two hundred expressions—thus, four hundred knowledges should be known.

“Atthasataṃ dhammasataṃ, dve niruttisatāni;

Cattāri ñāṇasatāni, atthavase pakaraṇe”ti.

“A hundred meanings, a hundred teachings, two hundred expressions; four hundred knowledges, in the Exposition on the Grounds for Benefits.”

Iti hi yaṃ vuttaṃ, idametaṃ paṭicca vuttanti.

Thus indeed, what was said, this was said in reference to that.

Iti samantapāsādikāya vinayasaṃvaṇṇanāya

Thus, in the Samantapāsādikā, the commentary on the Vinaya,

Mahāvaggavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Mahāvagga is concluded.

Paṭhamagāthāsaṅgaṇikaṃ

The First Collection of Verses.

Sattanagaresu paññattasikkhāpadavaṇṇanā

The Explanation of the Training Rules Laid Down in the Seven Towns.

335. Ekaṃsaṃ [M 0.0187][V 0.0187] cīvaraṃ katvāti ekasmiṃ aṃsakūṭe cīvaraṃ katvā; sādhukaṃ uttarāsaṅgaṃ katvāti attho. Paggaṇhitvāna añjalinti dasanakhasamodhānasamujjalaṃ añjaliṃ ukkhipitvā. Āsīsamānarūpo vāti paccāsīsamānarūpo viya. Kissa tvaṃ idha māgatoti kena kāraṇena kimatthaṃ patthayamāno tvaṃ idha āgato. Ko evamāha? Sammāsambuddho. Kaṃ evamāha? Āyasmantaṃ upāliṃ. Iti āyasmā upāli bhagavantaṃ [P 7.1348] upasaṅkamitvā “dvīsu vinayesū”ti imaṃ gāthaṃ pucchi. Athassa bhagavā “bhaddako te ummaṅgo”tiādīni vatvā taṃ vissajjesi. Esa nayo sabbattha. Iti ime sabbapañhe buddhakāle upālitthero pucchi. Bhagavā byākāsi. Saṅgītikāle pana mahākassapatthero pucchi. Upālitthero byākāsi.

335. “Having made the robe one-shouldered” means having arranged the robe over one shoulder; the meaning is having properly prepared the upper robe. “Having raised the joined hands” means lifting up the joined hands, resplendent with the joining of the ten fingernails. “In the manner of one expecting” means as if in the manner of one expecting something. “Why have you come here?” For what reason, desiring what, have you come here? Who says this? The Perfectly Self-Enlightened One. To whom is this said? To the Venerable Upāli. Thus, the Venerable Upāli approached the Blessed One and asked this verse beginning “in the two Vinayas.” Then the Blessed One, having said to him, “Good is your question,” and so on, answered it. This is the method in all cases. Thus, all these questions were asked by the Elder Upāli during the Buddha’s time. The Blessed One explained them. However, during the time of the Council, the Elder Mahākassapa asked, and the Elder Upāli explained.

Tattha bhaddako te ummaṅgoti bhaddakā te pañhā; pañhā hi avijjandhakārato ummujjitvā ṭhitattā “ummaṅgo”ti vuccati. Tagghāti kāraṇatthe nipāto. Yasmā maṃ pucchasi, tasmā te ahamakkhissanti attho. Sampaṭicchanatthe vā, “tagghā”ti hi iminā vacanaṃ sampaṭicchitvā akkhissanti āha. “Samādahitvā visibbenti, sāmisena, sasitthaka”nti imāni tīṇiyeva sikkhāpadāni bhaggesu paññattāni.

Here, ‘bhaddako te ummaṅgo’ means your questions are excellent; for a question is called ‘ummaṅga’ because it stands having emerged from the darkness of ignorance. ‘Taggha’ is a particle in the sense of reason. The meaning is: because you ask me, therefore I will explain to you. Or, it is in the sense of acceptance; for having accepted the statement with ‘taggha,’ he said, 'I will explain.' ‘Having agreed and then unsewing,’ ‘with material gain,’ and ‘with leftovers’—these three training rules alone were laid down among the Bhaggas.

Catuvipattivaṇṇanā

The Explanation of the Four Transgressions.

336. Yaṃ taṃ pucchimhāti yaṃ tvaṃ apucchimhā. Akittayīti abhāsi. Noti amhākaṃ[V 0.0188]. Taṃ taṃ byākatanti yaṃ yaṃ puṭṭhaṃ, taṃ tadeva byākataṃ. Anaññathāti aññathā akatvā byākataṃ.

336. ‘What we asked you’ means ‘what we asked you’. ‘Akittayi’ means ‘he spoke’. ‘No’ means ‘to us’. ‘That, that was explained’ means ‘whatever was asked, that very thing was explained’. ‘Not otherwise’ means ‘explained without making it otherwise’.

Ye duṭṭhullā sā sīlavipattīti ettha kiñcāpi sīlavipatti nāma pañhe natthi, atha kho duṭṭhullaṃ vissajjetukāmatāyetaṃ vuttaṃ. Catūsu hi vipattīsu duṭṭhullaṃ ekāya vipattiyā saṅgahitaṃ, aduṭṭhullaṃ tīhi vipattīhi saṅgahitaṃ. Tasmā “ye duṭṭhullā sā sīlavipattī”ti vatvā tameva vitthārato dassetuṃ “pārājikaṃ saṅghādiseso sīlavipattīti vuccatī”ti āha.

Regarding the statement, ‘Those that are gross, that is a transgression of virtue’: here, although there is no mention of a transgression of virtue in the question, this was stated with the intention of explaining the gross offense. For among the four transgressions, the gross offense is included under one transgression, while the non-gross offense is included under three transgressions. Therefore, having stated, ‘Those that are gross, that is a transgression of virtue,’ to explain that in detail, he said, ‘A pārājika and a saṅghādisesa are called a transgression of virtue.’

Idāni [M 0.0188] tissannaṃ vipattīnaṃ vasena aduṭṭhullaṃ dassetuṃ “thullaccaya”ntiādimāha. Tattha yo cāyaṃ, akkosati hasādhippāyoti idaṃ dubbhāsitassa vatthudassanatthaṃ vuttaṃ.

Now, to show the non-gross offense by way of the three transgressions, he says, ‘a thullaccaya offense’ and so on. Here, the phrase ‘whoever reviles with the intent to mock’ was stated to illustrate the basis for the offense of wrong speech.

Abbhācikkhantīti “tathāhaṃ bhagavatā dhammaṃ desitaṃ ājānāmī”ti vadantā abbhācikkhanti.

‘They falsely accuse’ means that saying, ‘Thus do I understand the Dhamma as taught by the Blessed One,’ they falsely accuse.

Ayaṃ sā ājīvavipattisammatāti ayaṃ chahi sikkhāpadehi saṅgahitā ājīvavipatti nāma catutthā vipatti sammatāti ettāvatā “aduṭṭhulla”nti idaṃ vissajjitaṃ hoti.

‘This is considered the transgression of livelihood’ means: this transgression of livelihood, which is included in six training rules, is considered the fourth transgression. By this much, the question concerning the ‘non-gross’ has been answered.

Idāni “ye ca yāvatatiyakā”ti pañhaṃ vissajjetuṃ “ekādasā”tiādimāha.

Now, to answer the question, ‘And which are the cases of up to the third time?’, he says, ‘There are eleven’ and so on.

Chedanakādivaṇṇanā

The Explanation of Cutting Off, and So Forth

337. Yasmā pana “ye ca yāvatatiyakā”ti ayaṃ pañho “ekādasa yāvatatiyakā”ti evaṃ saṅkhāvasena vissajjito, tasmā saṅkhānusandhivaseneva “kati chedanakānī”tiādike aññe antarāpañhe pucchi. Tesaṃ vissajjanatthaṃ “cha chedanakānī”tiādi vuttaṃ. Tattha “ekaṃ [P 7.1349] bhedanakaṃ, ekaṃ uddālanakaṃ, sodasa jānanti paññattā”ti idameva apubbaṃ. Sesaṃ mahāvagge vibhattameva. Yaṃ panetaṃ apubbaṃ tattha ekaṃ bhedanakanti sūcigharaṃ. Ekaṃ uddālanakanti tūlonaddhamañcapīṭhaṃ. Sodasāti soḷasa. Jānanti paññattāti “jāna”nti evaṃ vatvā paññattā, te evaṃ veditabbā – “jānaṃ saṅghikaṃ lābhaṃ pariṇataṃ [V 0.0189] attano pariṇāmeyya, jānaṃ pubbupagataṃ bhikkhuṃ anupakhajja nisajjaṃ kappeyya, jānaṃ sappāṇakaṃ udakaṃ tiṇaṃ vā mattikaṃ vā siñceyya vā siñcāpeyya vā, jānaṃ bhikkhuniparipācitaṃ piṇḍapātaṃ bhuñjeyya, jānaṃ āsādanāpekkho bhuttasmiṃ pācittiyaṃ, jānaṃ sappāṇakaṃ udakaṃ paribhuñjeyya, jānaṃ yathādhammaṃ nihatādhikaraṇaṃ, jānaṃ duṭṭhullaṃ āpattiṃ paṭicchādeyya, jānaṃ ūnavīsativassaṃ puggalaṃ upasampādeyya, jānaṃ theyyasatthena saddhiṃ, jānaṃ tathāvādinā bhikkhunā akatānudhammena, jānaṃ tathānāsitaṃ samaṇuddesaṃ, jānaṃ saṅghikaṃ lābhaṃ pariṇataṃ puggalassa pariṇāmeyya, jānaṃ pārājikaṃ dhammaṃ ajjhāpannaṃ bhikkhuniṃ neva attanā paṭicodeyya, jānaṃ coriṃ vajjhaṃ viditaṃ anapaloketvā, jānaṃ sabhikkhukaṃ ārāmaṃ anāpucchā paviseyyā”ti.

337. Furthermore, since the question, ‘And which are the cases of up to the third time?’, was answered by way of enumeration as ‘There are eleven cases of up to the third time’, therefore, following only the connection of enumeration, other intermediate questions such as ‘How many cutting offenses?’ were asked. To answer them, the passage beginning with ‘There are six cutting offenses’ was stated. Therein, ‘one splitting offense, one tearing offense, sixteen are prescribed with the word ‘knowing’’—only this is new. The rest is already explained in the Mahāvagga. As for this new part, therein, ‘one splitting offense’ is the training rule concerning a needle-case. ‘One tearing offense’ is the training rule concerning a couch or bench stuffed with cotton. ‘Sodasa’ means sixteen. ‘Prescribed with the word ‘knowing’’ means prescribed by saying “knowing”; they should be understood thus: ‘Knowing, one might divert for oneself a gain intended for the Saṅgha; knowing, one might encroach upon a previously arrived monk and take a seat; knowing, one might sprinkle or cause to be sprinkled water with living beings, or grass or clay; knowing, one might eat almsfood arranged by a bhikkhunī; knowing, expecting to be reproached after having eaten, one commits a pācittiya; knowing, one might use water with living beings; knowing, one might re-open a legal question settled according to the Dhamma; knowing, one might conceal a gross offense; knowing, one might give the higher ordination to a person under twenty years of age; knowing, one might travel together with a band of thieves; knowing, one might associate with a monk who speaks thus and has not acted in accordance with the Dhamma; knowing, one might attend to a novice who has been so banished; knowing, one might divert for an individual a gain intended for the Saṅgha; knowing, one might not oneself reprove a bhikkhunī who has committed a pārājika offense; knowing, one might give ordination to a known and convicted female thief without having obtained permission; knowing, one might enter a monastery where there are monks without having asked permission’—these are they.

Asādhāraṇādivaṇṇanā

The Explanation of the Uncommon, and So Forth

338. Idāni [M 0.0189] “sādhāraṇaṃ asādhāraṇa”nti imaṃ purimapañhaṃ vissajjento “vīsaṃ dve satānī”tiādimāha. Tattha bhikkhunīhi asādhāraṇesu cha saṅghādisesāti vissaṭṭhi, kāyasaṃsaggo, duṭṭhullaṃ, attakāma, kuṭi, vihāroti. Dve aniyatehi aṭṭhāti dvīhi aniyatehi saddhiṃ aṭṭha ime.

338. Now, answering this previous question, ‘common and uncommon,’ he said the passage beginning with ‘two hundred and twenty.’ Therein, among the rules not shared with bhikkhunīs, the six Saṅghādisesas are: emission of semen, physical contact, lewd speech, serving one's own desires, a hut, and a dwelling. Together with the two Aniyata rules, these are eight.

Nissaggiyāni dvādasāti –

There are twelve rules entailing forfeiture:

Dhovanañca paṭiggaho, koseyyasuddhadvebhāgā;

Chabbassāni nisīdanaṃ, dve lomā paṭhamo patto;

Vassikā āraññakena cāti – ime dvādasa.

The rule on having an old robe washed, and the rule on accepting a robe; the rules on silk, pure black wool, and two parts; the rule on six years, and the rule on a felt sitting-rug; the two rules on goat's wool, and the first rule on a bowl; and the rule on the rains-bathing cloth, together with the rule for a forest-dweller—these are the twelve.

Dvevīsati khuddakāti –

There are twenty-two minor rules:

Sakalo bhikkhunīvaggo, paramparañca bhojanaṃ;

Anatirittaṃ abhihaṭaṃ, paṇītañca acelakaṃ;

Ūnaṃ duṭṭhullachādanaṃ.

The entire Bhikkhunovādaka chapter, and eating in succession; food that is not left over, food that is brought, and choice food; the rule concerning a naked ascetic; ordaining one who is under age, and concealing a gross offense.

Mātugāmena [V 0.0190] saddhiñca, yā ca anikkhantarājake;

Santaṃ [P 7.1350] bhikkhuṃ anāpucchā, vikāle gāmappavesanā.

And traveling together with a woman; and the rule concerning when the king has not departed; entering a village at the wrong time without asking a monk who is present.

Nisīdane ca yā sikkhā, vassikā yā ca sāṭikā;

Dvāvīsati imā sikkhā, khuddakesu pakāsitāti.

And the training rule concerning a sitting-rug, and that concerning the rains-bathing cloth; these twenty-two training rules are declared among the minor ones.

Bhikkhūhi asādhāraṇesupi saṅghamhā dasa nissareti “saṅghamhā nissārīyatī”ti evaṃ vibhaṅge vuttā, mātikāyaṃ pana “nissāraṇīyaṃ saṅghādisesa”nti evaṃ āgatā dasa. Nissaggiyāni dvādasāti bhikkhunivibhaṅge vibhattāni nissaggiyāneva. Khuddakāpi tattha vibhattakhuddakā eva. Tathā cattāro pāṭidesanīyā, iti satañceva tiṃsañca sikkhā vibhaṅge bhikkhunīnaṃ bhikkhūhi asādhāraṇā. Sesaṃ imasmiṃ sādhāraṇāsādhāraṇavissajjane uttānameva.

Also among the rules for bhikkhunīs not shared with bhikkhus, there are ten Saṅghādisesas that require expulsion. They are stated in the Vibhaṅga as ‘is expelled from the Saṅgha,’ but in the Mātikā, ten are given as ‘Saṅghādisesa requiring expulsion.’ As for the twelve rules entailing forfeiture, they are explained in the Bhikkhunīvibhaṅga as simply rules entailing forfeiture. The minor rules, too, are explained there as simply minor rules. Likewise, the four Pāṭidesanīya rules are explained there. Thus, one hundred and thirty training rules in the Vibhaṅga are for bhikkhunīs and are not shared with bhikkhus. The remainder in this answer on shared and unshared is self-evident.

Idāni vipattiyo ca “yehi samathehi sammantī”ti idaṃ pañhaṃ vissajjento aṭṭheva pārājikātiādimāha. Tattha durāsadāti iminā tesaṃ sappaṭibhayataṃ [M 0.0190] dasseti. Kaṇhasappādayo viya hi ete durāsadā durūpagamanā durāsajjanā, āpajjiyamānā mūlacchedāya saṃvattanti. Tālavatthusamūpamāti sabbaṃ tālaṃ uddharitvā tālassa vatthumattakaraṇena samūpamā. Yathā vatthumattakato tālo na puna pākatiko hoti, evaṃ na puna pākatikā honti.

Now, answering this question, ‘And by which settlements are the offenses settled?’, the Blessed One said the passage beginning with ‘the eight Pārājikas.’ Therein, by the word ‘difficult to approach,’ he shows their perilousness. For, like black snakes and so forth, these are difficult to approach, difficult to draw near to, difficult to associate with; when they are fallen into, they lead to the cutting of the root. The simile of the palm-stump is like making the entire palm tree into a mere base after having uprooted it. Just as a palm tree made into a mere base cannot become normal again, so too, they cannot become normal again.

Evaṃ sādhāraṇaṃ upamaṃ dassetvā puna ekekassa vuttaṃ upamaṃ dassento paṇḍupalāsotiādimāha. Aviruḷhī bhavanti teti yathā ete paṇḍupalāsādayo punaharitādibhāvena aviruḷhidhammā honti; evaṃ pārājikāpi puna pakatisīlābhāvena aviruḷhidhammā hontīti attho. Ettāvatā “vipattiyo ca yehi samathehi sammantī”ti ettha imā tāva aṭṭha pārājikavipattiyo kehici samathehi na sammantīti evaṃ dassitaṃ hoti. Yā pana vipattiyo sammanti, tā dassetuṃ tevīsati saṅghādisesātiādi vuttaṃ. Tattha tīhi samathehīti sabbasaṅgāhikavacanametaṃ. Saṅghādisesā hi dvīhi samathehi sammanti, na tiṇavatthārakena. Sesā tīhipi sammanti.

Having thus shown a simile common to all, then, to show the simile spoken for each one individually, he said the passage beginning with ‘a yellow leaf.’ ‘They do not grow again’ means: just as these yellow leaves and so on are of a nature not to grow again into a state of greenness, so too, those who have committed a Pārājika are of a nature not to grow again, due to the absence of their original virtue; this is the meaning. By this much, in the context of ‘And by which settlements are the offenses settled?’, it has been shown that these eight Pārājika offenses are not settled by any settlements whatsoever.
