But to show the offenses that are settled, the passage beginning with ‘twenty-three Saṅghādisesas’ was stated. Therein, the phrase ‘by three settlements’ is an all-inclusive statement. For Saṅghādisesas are settled by two settlements, not by the covering with grass. The remaining offenses are settled by all three.

Dve uposathā dve pavāraṇāti idaṃ bhikkhūnañca bhikkhunīnañca vasena vuttaṃ. Vibhattimattadassaneneva [V 0.0191] cetaṃ vuttaṃ, na samathehi vūpasamanavasena. Bhikkhuuposatho, bhikkhuniuposatho, bhikkhupavāraṇā, bhikkhunipavāraṇāti imāpi hi catasso vibhattiyo[P 7.1351]; vibhajanānīti attho. Cattāri kammānīti adhammenavaggādīni uposathakammāni. Pañceva uddesā caturo bhavanti anaññathāti bhikkhūnaṃ pañca uddesā bhikkhunīnaṃ caturo bhavanti, aññathā na bhavanti; imā aparāpi vibhattiyo. Āpattikkhandhā ca bhavanti satta, adhikaraṇāni cattārīti imā pana vibhattiyo samathehi sammanti, tasmā sattahi samathehītiādimāha. Atha vā “dve uposathā dve pavāraṇā cattāri kammāni pañceva uddesā caturo bhavanti, anaññathā”ti imāpi catasso vibhattiyo nissāya “nassantete vinassantete”tiādinā nayena yā āpattiyo āpajjanti, tā yasmā vuttappakāreheva samathehi sammanti, tasmā taṃmūlakānaṃ āpattīnaṃ samathadassanatthampi tā vibhattiyo vuttāti veditabbā. Kiccaṃ ekenāti kiccādhikaraṇaṃ ekena samathena sammati.

The statement, 'Two Uposathas, two Pavāraṇās,' was spoken with reference to monks and nuns. And this was stated merely to show the classification, not for the purpose of showing the method of settling by the settlements. The Uposatha of monks, the Uposatha of nuns, the Pavāraṇā of monks, and the Pavāraṇā of nuns—indeed, these are also four classifications; the meaning is 'divisions.' As for 'four acts,' the Uposatha acts beginning with adhammenavagga are shown. As for 'five recitations, and four there are, not otherwise,' for monks there are five recitations and for nuns there are four; there are not otherwise. These are also other classifications. As for 'there are seven groups of offenses and four legal cases,' these classifications, however, are settled by the settlements; therefore, He said, 'by seven settlements,' and so on. Or alternatively, relying on these four classifications—'two Uposathas, two Pavāraṇās, four acts, five recitations, and four there are, not otherwise'—whatever offenses are committed, in the manner of 'they perish, they are destroyed,' etc., since those offenses are settled by the settlements of the aforementioned kinds, it should be understood that those classifications were also stated for the purpose of showing the settlement of offenses that have those as their root. As for 'a matter by one,' a legal case concerning duties (kiccādhikaraṇa) is settled by one settlement.

Pārājikādiāpattivaṇṇanā

Explanation of the Offenses Beginning with Pārājika

339. Evaṃ [M 0.0191] pucchānukkamena sabbapañhe vissajjetvā idāni “āpattikkhandhā ca bhavanti sattā”ti ettha saṅgahitaāpattikkhandhānaṃ paccekaṃ nibbacanamattaṃ dassento pārājikantiādimāha. Tattha pārājikanti gāthāya ayamattho – yadidaṃ puggalāpattisikkhāpadapārājikesu āpattipārājikaṃ nāma vuttaṃ, taṃ āpajjanto puggalo yasmā parājito parājayamāpanno saddhammā cuto paraddho bhaṭṭho niraṅkato ca hoti, anihate tasmiṃ puggale puna uposathappavāraṇādibhedo saṃvāso natthi. Tenetaṃ iti vuccatīti tena kāraṇena etaṃ āpattipārājikanti vuccati. Ayañhettha saṅkhepattho – yasmā parājito hoti tena, tasmā etaṃ pārājikanti vuccati.

339. Thus, having answered all the questions in sequence of inquiry, now, regarding the statement, 'there are seven aggregates of offenses,' to show the individual etymology of the aggregates of offenses included therein, He spoke the passage beginning with pārājikaṃ. Therein, this is the meaning of the verse on pārājika: That which, among the pārājikas of person, offense, and training-rule, is called the pārājika that is an offense—because a person who commits it is defeated, has come to defeat, has fallen from the good Dhamma, has failed, has fallen away, and is made invalid, and for that person, even if not expelled, there is again no communion which has the distinctions of Uposatha, Pavāraṇā, and so on. For that reason, this is called the pārājika that is an offense. And this is the concise meaning here: because one is defeated by it, therefore it is called pārājika.

Dutiyagāthāyapi byañjanaṃ anādiyitvā atthamattameva dassetuṃ saṅghova deti parivāsantiādi vuttaṃ. Ayaṃ panettha attho – imaṃ āpattiṃ āpajjitvā vuṭṭhātukāmassa yaṃ taṃ āpattivuṭṭhānaṃ tassa ādimhi ceva parivāsadānatthāya ādito sese majjhe mānattadānatthāya mūlāyapaṭikassanena vā saha mānattadānatthāya, avasāne abbhānatthāya ca saṅgho icchitabbo, na [V 0.0192] hettha ekampi kammaṃ vinā saṅghena sakkā kātunti saṅgho, ādimhi ceva sese ca icchitabbo assāti saṅghādiseso.

In the second verse also, without taking the wording seriously, to show only the meaning, the passage beginning with 'the Sangha gives probation' was spoken. This is the meaning here: for a person who, having committed this offense, desires to be reinstated, for that formal act of reinstatement, the Sangha is required both at the beginning for the purpose of giving probation; in the middle, which is the remainder from the beginning, for the purpose of giving penance, or for giving penance together with being sent back to the beginning; and at the end for the purpose of rehabilitation. For here, not even one formal act can be done without the Sangha. Thus, because the Sangha is required both at the beginning and in the remainder, it is a saṅghādisesa.

Tatiyagāthāya aniyato na niyatoti yasmā na niyato, tasmā aniyato ayamāpattikkhandhoti attho. Kiṃ kāraṇā na niyatoti? Anekaṃsikataṃ [P 7.1352] padaṃ, yasmā idaṃ sikkhāpadaṃ anekaṃsena katanti attho. Kathaṃ anekaṃsena? Tiṇṇamaññataraṃ ṭhānaṃ, tiṇṇaṃ dhammānaṃ aññatarena kāretabboti hi tattha vuttaṃ, tasmā “aniyato”ti pavuccati, so āpattikkhandho aniyatoti vuccati. Yathā ca tiṇṇaṃ aññataraṃ ṭhānaṃ, evaṃ dvinnaṃ dhammānaṃ aññataraṃ ṭhānaṃ yattha vuttaṃ, sopi aniyato eva.

In the third verse, 'aniyato' means 'not fixed.' Because it is not fixed, therefore this aggregate of offenses is aniyata—this is the meaning. For what reason is it not fixed? The training-rule is made to be indefinite. Because this training-rule is made to be indefinite—this is the meaning. How is it indefinite? It is one ground or another of three. For it is said there, 'it should be dealt with by one or another of three offenses.' Therefore, it is called aniyata; that aggregate of offenses is called aniyata. And just as it is 'one ground or another of three,' so too where 'one ground or another of two offenses' is stated, that too is indeed aniyata.

Catutthagāthāya accayo tena samo natthīti desanāgāminīsu accayesu tena samo thūlo accayo natthi, tenetaṃ iti vuccati; thūlattā accayassa etaṃ thullaccayanti vuccatīti attho.

In the fourth verse, 'there is no offense equal to it' means that among the offenses that are to be confessed, there is no gross offense equal to it; for that reason it is called this. Because of the grossness of the offense, it is called thullaccaya—this is the meaning.

Pañcamagāthāya [M 0.0192] nissajjitvāna deseti tenetanti nissajjitvā desetabbato nissaggiyanti vuccatīti attho.

In the fifth verse, 'having forfeited, one confesses; therefore, this' means that because it ought to be confessed after having been forfeited, it is called nissaggiya—this is the meaning.

Chaṭṭhagāthāya pāteti kusalaṃ dhammanti sañcicca āpajjantassa kusaladhammasaṅkhātaṃ kusalacittaṃ pāteti, tasmā pāteti cittanti pācittiyaṃ. Yaṃ pana cittaṃ pāteti, taṃ yasmā ariyamaggaṃ aparajjhati, cittasammohakāraṇañca hoti, tasmā “ariyamaggaṃ aparajjhati, cittasammohanaṭṭhāna”nti ca vuttaṃ.

In the sixth verse, 'it causes a wholesome state to fall' means that for one who commits it intentionally, it causes the wholesome consciousness, known as a wholesome state, to fall; therefore, because 'it causes the mind to fall,' it is a pācittiya. Moreover, the offense that causes the mind to fall, because it offends against the noble path and is a cause of mental confusion, it is therefore also said, 'it offends against the noble path' and 'it is a ground for mental confusion.'

Pāṭidesanīyagāthāsu “gārayhaṃ āvuso dhammaṃ āpajji”nti vuttagārayhabhāvakāraṇadassanatthameva bhikkhu aññātako santotiādi vuttaṃ. Paṭidesetabbato pana sā āpatti pāṭidesanīyāti vuccati.

In the Pāṭidesanīya verses, the passage beginning with 'a monk, being unrelated,' was spoken only for the purpose of showing the reason for the state of blameworthiness mentioned in the statement, 'Friend, I have committed a blameworthy act.' But because that offense ought to be confessed individually, it is called pāṭidesanīya.

Dukkaṭagāthāya aparaddhaṃ viraddhañca khalitanti sabbametaṃ “yañca dukkaṭa”nti ettha vuttassa dukkaṭassa pariyāyavacanaṃ. Yañhi duṭṭhu kataṃ virūpaṃ vā kataṃ, taṃ dukkaṭaṃ. Taṃ panetaṃ yathā satthārā vuttaṃ; evaṃ akatattā aparaddhaṃ, kusalaṃ virajjhitvā pavattattā viraddhaṃ, ariyavattapaṭipadaṃ anāruḷhattā [V 0.0193] khalitaṃ. Yaṃ manusso kareti idaṃ panassa opammanidassanaṃ. Tassattho – yathā hi yaṃ loke manusso āvi vā yadi vā raho pāpaṃ karoti, taṃ dukkaṭanti pavedenti; evamidampi buddhappaṭikuṭṭhena lāmakabhāvena pāpaṃ, tasmā dukkaṭanti veditabbaṃ.

In the Dukkaṭa verse, the terms 'done wrongly' (aparaddhaṃ), 'failed' (viraddhaṃ), and 'stumbled' (khalitaṃ)—all these are synonyms for the dukkaṭa spoken of here in 'and what is a dukkaṭa.' For whatever is badly done or improperly done, that is a dukkaṭa. But this, because it was not done in the way spoken by the Teacher, is 'done wrongly'; because it occurs having failed in what is wholesome, it is 'failed'; because of not having ascended the path of practice of the noble ones' conduct, it is 'stumbled.' The phrase 'what a person does,' however, is an illustrative analogy for it. Its meaning is: For just as in the world, whatever evil a person does, whether openly or in secret, people make it known as a 'wrongdoing' (dukkaṭa); so too this, because it is censured by the Buddha and because of its inferior nature, is evil; therefore, it should be understood as a dukkaṭa.

Dubbhāsitagāthāya dubbhāsitaṃ durābhaṭṭhanti duṭṭhu ābhaṭṭhaṃ bhāsitaṃ lapitanti durābhaṭṭhaṃ. Yaṃ durābhaṭṭhaṃ, taṃ dubbhāsitanti attho. Kiñca bhiyyo? Saṃkiliṭṭhañca yaṃ padaṃ, saṃkiliṭṭhaṃ yasmā taṃ padaṃ hotīti attho. Tathā yañca viññū garahanti, yasmā ca naṃ [P 7.1353] viññū garahantīti attho. Tenetaṃ iti vuccatīti tena saṃkiliṭṭhabhāvena ca viññugarahanenāpi ca etaṃ iti vuccati; “dubbhāsita”nti evaṃ vuccatīti attho.

In the Dubbhāsita verse, regarding `dubbhāsitaṃ durābhaṭṭhaṃ`: `durābhaṭṭhaṃ` means badly uttered, spoken, or recited. What is badly uttered is `dubbhāsitaṃ`—this is the meaning. And what more? 'And what is a defiled word,' means because that word is defiled. Likewise, 'and what the wise censure,' means because the wise censure it. 'For that reason it is called thus' means that because of its defiled nature and also because of being censured by the wise, it is called thus; that is, it is called `dubbhāsitaṃ`—this is the meaning.

Sekhiyagāthāya “ādi cetaṃ caraṇañcā”tiādinā nayena sekhassa santakabhāvaṃ dīpeti. Tasmā sekhassa idaṃ sekhiyanti ayamettha saṅkhepattho. Idaṃ “garukalahukañcāpī”tiādipañhehi asaṅgahitassa “handa vākyaṃ suṇoma te”ti iminā pana āyācanavacanena saṅgahitassa atthassa dīpanatthaṃ vuttanti veditabbaṃ.

In the Sekhiya verse, by the method beginning with 'and this is the beginning and the practice,' it indicates the state of being the trainee's possession. Therefore, 'this belongs to the trainee,' hence it is a `sekhiya`—this is the concise meaning here. It should be known that this was said for the purpose of explaining the meaning of that which is not included by the questions beginning with 'and whether grave or light,' but which is included by this request-speech, 'Come, let us hear your statement.'

Channamativassatītiādimhipi [M 0.0193] eseva nayo. Tattha channamativassatīti gehaṃ tāva tiṇādīhi acchannaṃ ativassati. Idaṃ pana āpattisaṅkhātaṃ gehaṃ channaṃ ativassati; mūlāpattiñhi chādento aññaṃ navaṃ āpattiṃ āpajjati. Vivaṭaṃ nātivassatīti gehaṃ tāva avivaṭaṃ succhannaṃ nātivassati. Idaṃ pana āpattisaṅkhātaṃ gehaṃ vivaṭaṃ nātivassati; mūlāpattiñhi vivaranto desanāgāminiṃ desetvā vuṭṭhānagāminito vuṭṭhahitvā suddhante patiṭṭhāti. Āyatiṃ saṃvaranto aññaṃ āpattiṃ nāpajjati. Tasmā channaṃ vivarethāti tena kāraṇena desanāgāminiṃ desento vuṭṭhānagāminito ca vuṭṭhahanto channaṃ vivaretha. Evaṃ taṃ nātivassatīti evañcetaṃ vivaṭaṃ nātivassatīti attho.

In the verse beginning 'It leaks when covered,' this same method applies. There, regarding 'it leaks when covered': first, a house uncovered with grass and so forth leaks. But this house called an offense leaks when covered; for a monk covering up a root offense incurs another new offense. Regarding 'it does not leak when open': first, a house that is not open and is well-covered does not leak. But this house called an offense does not leak when open; for a monk revealing a root offense, having confessed a confessable offense and having emerged from an offense requiring emergence, is established in the state of purity. By restraining himself in the future, he does not incur another offense. Therefore, 'one should open the covered': for that reason, while confessing a confessable offense and emerging from an offense requiring emergence, one should open what is covered. 'Thus it does not leak' means: and so this, being open, does not leak—this is the meaning.

Gati migānaṃ pavananti ajjhokāse byagghādīhi paripātiyamānānaṃ migānaṃ pavanaṃ rukkhādigahanaṃ araññaṃ gati paṭisaraṇaṃ hoti, taṃ patvā te assāsanti. Eteneva nayena ākāso pakkhīnaṃ gati. Avassaṃ upagamanaṭṭhena pana vibhavo gati dhammānaṃ, sabbesampi saṅkhatadhammānaṃ vināsova tesaṃ gati. Na hi te vināsaṃ agacchantā ṭhātuṃ sakkonti. Sucirampi [V 0.0194] ṭhatvā pana nibbānaṃ arahato gati, khīṇāsavassa arahato anupādisesanibbānadhātu ekaṃsena gatīti attho.

“The forest is the destination for deer” means that for deer being pursued by tigers and so forth in the open, the forest—a thicket of trees and so forth, a wilderness—is their destination and refuge; having reached it, they find relief. In this same way, the sky is the destination for birds. But, in the sense of being inevitably approached, cessation is the destination for phenomena; indeed, the destruction of all conditioned phenomena is their destination. For they cannot endure without going to destruction. But, having endured even for a very long time, Nibbāna is the destination for the Arahant; for the Arahant whose taints are destroyed, the Nibbāna-element without residue remaining is certainly the destination—this is the meaning.

Paṭhamagāthāsaṅgaṇikavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The commentary on the first collection of verses is concluded.

Adhikaraṇabhedaṃ

The Division of Legal Issues

Ukkoṭanabhedādivaṇṇanā

Explanation of the Divisions of Overturning and so forth

340. Adhikaraṇabhede [P 7.1354][M 0.0194][V 0.0195] ime dasa ukkoṭāti adhikaraṇānaṃ ukkoṭetvā puna adhikaraṇaukkoṭena samathānaṃ ukkoṭaṃ dassetuṃ “vivādādhikaraṇaṃ ukkoṭento kati samathe ukkoṭetī”tiādimāha. Tattha vivādādhikaraṇaṃ ukkoṭento dve samathe ukkoṭetīti sammukhāvinayañca yebhuyyasikañca ime dve ukkoṭeti, paṭisedheti; paṭikkosatīti attho. Anuvādādhikaraṇaṃ ukkoṭento cattāroti sammukhāvinayaṃ, sativinayaṃ, amūḷhavinayaṃ, tassapāpiyasikanti ime cattāro samathe ukkoṭeti. Āpattādhikaraṇaṃ ukkoṭento tayoti sammukhāvinayaṃ, paṭiññātakaraṇaṃ, tiṇavatthārakanti ime tayo samathe ukkoṭeti. Kiccādhikaraṇaṃ ukkoṭento ekanti sammukhāvinayaṃ imaṃ ekaṃ samathaṃ ukkoṭeti.

340. In the section on the division of legal issues, having shown the ten types of overturning of legal issues with the words, 'these ten are overturnings,' in order to show again the overturning of settlements by means of overturning legal issues, he spoke the passage beginning with: “When overturning a legal issue of dispute, how many settlements does one overturn?” Therein, regarding “When overturning a legal issue of dispute, one overturns two settlements”: one overturns these two, the procedure in the presence and the majority decision. The meaning is that one prohibits, one objects. Regarding “When overturning a legal issue of accusation, four”: one overturns these four settlements: the procedure in the presence, the resolution by mindfulness, the resolution of non-delusion, and the procedure for one of bad character. Regarding “When overturning a legal issue of an offense, three”: one overturns these three settlements: the procedure in the presence, the procedure on acknowledgement, and the covering over with grass. Regarding “When overturning a legal issue of duties, one”: one overturns this one settlement, the procedure in the presence.

341. Kati ukkoṭātiādipucchānaṃ vissajjane pana dvādasasu ukkoṭesu akataṃ kammantiādayo tāva tayo ukkoṭā visesato dutiye anuvādādhikaraṇe labbhanti. Anihataṃ kammantiādayo tayo paṭhame vivādādhikaraṇe labbhanti. Avinicchitantiādayo tayo tatiye āpattādhikaraṇe labbhanti. Avūpasantantiādayo tayo catutthe kiccādhikaraṇe labbhanti; apica dvādasāpi ca ekekasmiṃ adhikaraṇe labbhantiyeva.

341. Now, in the answer to the questions beginning with, “How many overturnings?”, among the twelve overturnings, first, the three overturnings beginning with “the act was not done” are found specifically in the second, the legal issue of accusation. The three beginning with “the act was not refuted” are found in the first, the legal issue of dispute. The three beginning with “it was not decided” are found in the third, the legal issue of an offense. The three beginning with “it was not settled” are found in the fourth, the legal issue of duties. Moreover, all twelve are indeed found in each and every legal issue.

Tatthajātakaṃ adhikaraṇaṃ ukkoṭetīti yasmiṃ vihāre “mayhaṃ iminā patto gahito, cīvaraṃ gahita”ntiādinā nayena pattacīvarādīnaṃ atthāya adhikaraṇaṃ uppannaṃ hoti, tasmiṃyeva ca naṃ vihāre āvāsikā sannipatitvā “alaṃ āvuso”ti attapaccatthike saññāpetvā pāḷimuttakavinicchayeneva [V 0.0196] vūpasamenti, idaṃ tatthajātakaṃ adhikaraṇaṃ nāma. Yenāpi vinicchayena samitaṃ, sopi eko samathoyeva. Imaṃ ukkoṭentassāpi pācittiyaṃ.

Regarding one who overturns a legal issue that arose in that place: In whatever monastery a legal issue of accusation has arisen for the sake of a bowl, robe, or the like, in the manner of, “My bowl was taken by this one; my robe was taken,” and in that very monastery the resident monks, having assembled and made the adversaries understand by saying, “Enough, venerable sirs,” settle it solely by a decision independent of the Pāḷi Canon—this is called a legal issue that arose in that place. The decision by which it is settled is also a single type of settlement. For one who overturns this, there is a pācittiya offense.

Tatthajātakaṃ vūpasantanti sace pana taṃ adhikaraṇaṃ nevāsikā vūpasametuṃ na sakkonti, athañño vinayadharo āgantvā “kiṃ āvuso imasmiṃ [M 0.0195] vihāre uposatho vā pavāraṇā vā ṭhitā”ti pucchati, tehi ca tasmiṃ kāraṇe kathite taṃ adhikaraṇaṃ khandhakato ca parivārato ca suttena vinicchinitvā vūpasameti, idaṃ tatthajātakaṃ vūpasantaṃ nāma adhikaraṇaṃ. Etaṃ ukkoṭentassāpi pācittiyameva.

Regarding a legal issue that arose there and was settled: If, however, the resident monks are unable to settle that legal issue, then another Vinaya-expert comes and asks, “Venerable sirs, has the Uposatha or the Pavāraṇā been suspended in this monastery?” And when the reason is told by them, he settles that legal issue, having decided it by the Pāḷi text from the Khandhakas and the Parivāra. This is called a legal issue that arose there and was settled. For one who overturns this, there is also a pācittiya offense.

Antarāmaggeti te attapaccatthikā “na mayaṃ etassa vinicchaye tiṭṭhāma, nāyaṃ vinaye kusalo, asukasmiṃ [P 7.1355] nāma gāme vinayadharā therā vasanti, tattha gantvā vinicchinissāmā”ti gacchantā antarāmaggeyeva kāraṇaṃ sallakkhetvā aññamaññaṃ vā saññāpenti, aññe vā te bhikkhū nijjhāpenti, idampi vūpasantameva hoti. Evaṃ vūpasantaṃ antarāmagge adhikaraṇaṃ ukkoṭeti yo, tassāpi pācittiyameva.

Regarding a legal issue settled on the way: Those adversaries say, “We do not stand by this one’s decision; this one is not skilled in the Vinaya. In such-and-such village dwell elders who are Vinaya-experts; having gone there, we will have it decided.” While they are going, on the way itself, having considered the reason, they either make each other understand, or other monks cause them to reflect. This, too, is indeed settled. Whoever overturns a legal issue thus settled on the way, for him too there is a pācittiya offense.

Antarāmagge vūpasantanti na heva kho pana aññamaññaṃ saññattiyā vā sabhāgabhikkhunijjhāpanena vā vūpasantaṃ hoti; apica kho paṭipathaṃ āgacchanto eko vinayadharo disvā “kattha āvuso gacchathā”ti pucchitvā “asukaṃ nāma gāmaṃ, iminā nāma kāraṇenā”ti vutte “alaṃ, āvuso, kiṃ tattha gatenā”ti tattheva dhammena vinayena taṃ adhikaraṇaṃ vūpasameti, idaṃ antarāmagge vūpasantaṃ nāma. Etaṃ ukkoṭentassāpi pācittiyameva.

Regarding a legal issue settled on the way: It is not, however, settled by making each other understand or by the contemplation caused by like-minded monks. But rather, a certain Vinaya-expert coming along the path sees them and, having asked, “Venerable sirs, where are you going?” and when it is said, “To such-and-such village, for such-and-such reason,” he says, “Enough, venerable sirs. What is the use of having gone there?” and right there he settles that legal issue by the Dhamma and the Vinaya. This is called a legal issue settled on the way. For one who overturns this, there is also a pācittiya offense.

Tattha gatanti sace pana “alaṃ, āvuso, kiṃ tattha gatenā”ti vuccamānāpi “mayaṃ tattheva gantvā vinicchayaṃ pāpessāmā”ti vinayadharassa vacanaṃ anādiyitvā gacchantiyeva, gantvā sabhāgānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ etamatthaṃ ārocenti. Sabhāgā bhikkhū “alaṃ, āvuso, saṅghasannipātaṃ nāma garuka”nti tattheva nisīdāpetvā vinicchinitvā saññāpenti, idampi vūpasantameva hoti. Evaṃ vūpasantaṃ tattha gataṃ adhikaraṇaṃ ukkoṭeti yo, tassāpi pācittiyameva.

Regarding a legal issue settled after having gone there: If, however, although being told, “Enough, venerable sirs, what is the use of having gone there?” they, not accepting the word of the Vinaya-expert, just keep going, saying, “We, having gone right there, will cause a decision to be reached.” Having gone, they inform the like-minded monks of this matter. The like-minded monks, saying, “Enough, venerable sirs, a gathering of the Saṅgha is a heavy burden,” having made them sit right there and having decided the matter, make them understand. This, too, is indeed settled. Whoever overturns a legal issue that, having gone there, was settled in this way, for him too there is a pācittiya offense.

Tattha [V 0.0197] gataṃ vūpasantanti na heva kho pana sabhāgabhikkhūnaṃ saññattiyā vūpasantaṃ hoti; apica kho saṅghaṃ sannipātetvā ārocitaṃ saṅghamajjhe vinayadharā vūpasamenti, idaṃ tattha gataṃ vūpasantaṃ nāma. Etaṃ ukkoṭentassāpi pācittiyameva.

Regarding a legal issue that, having gone there, was settled: It is not, however, settled by the understanding caused by like-minded monks. But rather, having caused the Saṅgha to assemble, the Vinaya-experts settle the announced legal issue in the midst of the Saṅgha. This is called a legal issue that, having gone there, was settled. For one who overturns this, there is also a pācittiya offense.

Sativinayanti khīṇāsavassa dinnaṃ sativinayaṃ ukkoṭeti, pācittiyameva. Ummattakassa dinne amūḷhavinaye pāpussannassa dinnāya tassapāpiyasikāyapi eseva nayo.

Regarding the resolution by mindfulness: If one overturns the resolution by mindfulness given to one whose taints are destroyed, there is a pācittiya offense. This is also the rule for the resolution of non-delusion given to an insane person, and for the procedure for one of bad character given to one who abounds in evil.

Tiṇavatthārakaṃ [M 0.0196] ukkoṭetīti saṅghena tiṇavatthārakasamathena vūpasamite adhikaraṇe “āpatti nāma ekaṃ bhikkhuṃ upasaṅkamitvā ukkuṭikaṃ nisīditvā añjaliṃ paggahetvā desiyamānā vuṭṭhāti, yaṃ panetaṃ niddāyantassāpi āpattivuṭṭhānaṃ nāma, etaṃ mayhaṃ na khamatī”ti evaṃ vadantopi tiṇavatthārakaṃ ukkoṭeti nāma, tassāpi pācittiyameva.

Regarding one who overturns the covering over with grass: When a legal issue has been settled by the Saṅgha with the settlement of covering over with grass, if one says, “One rises from an offense, it is said, when, having approached a single monk, having sat squatting, and having raised the joined palms, it is confessed. But as for this so-called ‘rising from an offense,’ which occurs even for one who is drowsy, this is not agreeable to me”—one who speaks thus is called one who overturns the covering over with grass. For him too, there is a pācittiya offense.

Chandāgatiṃ gacchanto adhikaraṇaṃ ukkoṭetīti vinayadharo hutvā attano upajjhāyādīnaṃ [P 7.1356] atthāya “adhammaṃ dhammo”tiādīni dīpetvā pubbe vinicchitaṃ adhikaraṇaṃ dvādasasu ukkoṭesu yena kenaci ukkoṭento chandāgatiṃ gacchanto adhikaraṇaṃ ukkoṭeti nāma. Dvīsu pana attapaccatthikesu ekasmiṃ “anatthaṃ me acarī”tiādinā nayena samuppannāghāto, tassa parājayaṃ āropanatthaṃ “adhammaṃ dhammo”tiādīni dīpetvā pubbe vinicchitaṃ adhikaraṇaṃ dvādasasu ukkoṭesu yena kenaci ukkoṭento dosāgatiṃ gacchanto adhikaraṇaṃ ukkoṭeti nāma. Mando pana momūho momūhattā eva “adhammaṃ dhammo”tiādīni dīpetvā vuttanayeneva ukkoṭento mohāgatiṃ gacchanto adhikaraṇaṃ ukkoṭeti nāma. Sace pana dvīsu attapaccatthikesu eko visamāni kāyakammādīni gahanamicchādiṭṭhiṃ balavante ca pakkhantariye abhiññāte bhikkhū nissitattā visamanissito gahananissito balavanissito ca hoti, tassa bhayena “ayaṃ me jīvitantarāyaṃ vā brahmacariyantarāyaṃ vā kareyyā”ti “adhammaṃ dhammo”tiādīni dīpetvā vuttanayeneva ukkoṭento bhayāgatiṃ gacchanto adhikaraṇaṃ ukkoṭeti nāma.

Regarding 'overturning a legal issue by going to partiality': A Vinaya-expert who, for the sake of his own preceptor and others, shows that 'what is not Dhamma is Dhamma,' etc., and overturns a previously decided legal issue by any of the twelve methods of overturning—this is called overturning a legal issue by going to partiality. Furthermore, if, among two personal enemies, hatred arises in one of them with the thought, 'He has done me harm,' etc., and in order to bring about his defeat, he shows that 'what is not Dhamma is Dhamma,' etc., and overturns a previously decided legal issue by any of the twelve methods of overturning—this is called overturning a legal issue by going to aversion. A dull, utterly bewildered person who, simply due to being utterly bewildered, shows that 'what is not Dhamma is Dhamma,' etc., and overturns in the manner stated above—this is called overturning a legal issue by going to delusion. But if, among two personal enemies, one is supported by monks who are known for unrighteous bodily actions etc., who hold a tangled wrong view, and who are powerful and belong to another faction, thus being supported by the unrighteous, supported by the tangled, and supported by the powerful—then, out of fear, thinking, 'This one might create a danger to my life or to my holy life,' he shows that 'what is not Dhamma is Dhamma,' etc., and overturns in the manner stated above—this is called overturning a legal issue by going to fear.

Tadahupasampannoti eko sāmaṇero byatto hoti bahussuto, so vinicchaye parājayaṃ patvā maṅkubhūte bhikkhū disvā pucchati “kasmā maṅkubhūtātthā”ti? Te tassa taṃ adhikaraṇaṃ ārocenti. So te evaṃ vadeti “hotu, bhante, maṃ upasampādetha, ahaṃ taṃ adhikaraṇaṃ vūpasamessāmī”ti[V 0.0198]. Te taṃ upasampādenti. So dutiyadivase bheriṃ paharitvā saṅghaṃ sannipāteti. Tato bhikkhūhi “kena saṅgho sannipātito”ti vutte “mayā”ti vadati. “Kasmā sannipātito”ti? “Hiyyo adhikaraṇaṃ dubbinicchitaṃ, tamahaṃ vinicchinissāmī”ti. “Tvaṃ pana hiyyo kuhiṃ gato”ti? “Anupasampannomhi, bhante, ajja pana upasampannomhī”ti. So vattabbo “idaṃ āvuso tumhādisānaṃ bhagavatā sikkhāpadaṃ paññattaṃ, ‘tadahupasampanno [M 0.0197] ukkoṭeti, ukkoṭanakaṃ pācittiya’nti, gaccha āpattiṃ desehī”ti. Āgantukepi eseva nayo.

Regarding 'one ordained on that day': A certain novice was clever and very learned. Seeing the monks who had become dejected upon being defeated in a decision, he asked, 'Why are you dejected?' They explained the legal issue to him. He said to them, 'Let it be, venerable sirs. Ordain me, and I will settle that legal issue.' They ordained him. On the second day, he beat the drum and assembled the Saṅgha. When the monks asked, 'By whom has the Saṅgha been assembled?' he replied, 'By me.' 'For what reason has it been assembled?' 'Yesterday the legal issue was badly decided; I will decide it.' 'But where were you yesterday?' 'I was unordained, venerable sirs, but today I am ordained.' He should be told: 'Friend, for those like you the Blessed One has laid down this training rule: "One ordained on that day overturns; for overturning, it is a pācittiya offense." Go, confess your offense.' The same principle applies to a newcomer.

Kārakoti ekaṃ saṅghena saddhiṃ adhikaraṇaṃ vinicchinitvā pariveṇagataṃ parājitā bhikkhū vadanti “kissa, bhante, tumhehi evaṃ vinicchitaṃ adhikaraṇaṃ, nanu evaṃ vinicchinitabba”nti. So “kasmā paṭhamaṃyeva evaṃ na vaditthā”ti taṃ [P 7.1357] adhikaraṇaṃ ukkoṭeti. Evaṃ yo kārako ukkoṭeti, tassāpi ukkoṭanakaṃ pācittiyaṃ. Chandadāyakoti eko adhikaraṇavinicchaye chandaṃ datvā sabhāge bhikkhū parājayaṃ patvā āgate maṅkubhūte disvā “sve dāni ahaṃ vinicchinissāmī”ti saṅghaṃ sannipātetvā “kasmā sannipātesī”ti vutte “hiyyo adhikaraṇaṃ dubbinicchitaṃ, tamahaṃ ajja vinicchinissāmī”ti. “Hiyyo pana tvaṃ kattha gato”ti. “Chandaṃ datvā nisinnomhī”ti. So vattabbo “idaṃ āvuso tumhādisānaṃ bhagavatā sikkhāpadaṃ paññattaṃ, ‘chandadāyako ukkoṭeti, ukkoṭanakaṃ pācittiya’nti, gaccha āpattiṃ desehī”ti.

Regarding 'the participant': The defeated monks say to a certain monk who, having decided a legal issue together with the Saṅgha, had gone to his monastic dwelling, 'Venerable sir, why did you decide the legal issue in this way? Should it not have been decided thus?' He replies, 'Why did you not speak thus right from the beginning?' and overturns that legal issue. Thus, for whichever participant overturns it, the overturning is a pācittiya offense. Regarding 'the one who gives consent': A certain monk, having given his consent in the decision of a legal issue, sees his fellow monks who have returned defeated and dejected, and says, 'Now, tomorrow I will decide it.' He assembles the Saṅgha. When asked, 'Why have you assembled the Saṅgha?' he replies, 'Yesterday the legal issue was badly decided; today I will decide it.' 'But where were you yesterday?' 'Having given my consent, I remained apart.' He should be told: 'Friend, for those like you the Blessed One has laid down this training rule: "The one who gives consent overturns; for overturning, it is a pācittiya offense." Go, confess your offense.'

Adhikaraṇanidānādivaṇṇanā

The Explanation of the Source of Legal Issues, and so on

342. Vivādādhikaraṇaṃ kiṃnidānantiādīsu kiṃ nidānamassāti kiṃnidānaṃ. Ko samudayo assāti kiṃsamudayaṃ. Kā jāti assāti kiṃjātikaṃ. Ko pabhavo assāti kiṃpabhavaṃ. Ko sambhāro assāti kiṃsambhāraṃ. Kiṃ samuṭṭhānaṃ assāti kiṃsamuṭṭhānaṃ. Sabbānetāni kāraṇavevacanāniyeva.

342. In such phrases as 'Of what source is a legal issue of dispute?': 'What is its source?' means 'what-sourced'. 'What is its origin?' means 'what-originated'. 'What is its birth?' means 'what-born'. 'What is its provenance?' means 'what-provenanced'. 'What is its constituent?' means 'what-constituted'. 'What is its arising?' means 'what-arisen'. All these are just synonyms for 'cause'.

Vivādanidānantiādīsupi aṭṭhārasabhedakaravatthusaṅkhāto vivādo nidānametassāti vivādanidānaṃ. Vivādaṃ nissāya uppajjanakavivādavasenetaṃ vuttaṃ. Anuvādo nidānaṃ assāti anuvādanidānaṃ[V 0.0199]. Idampi anuvādaṃ nissāya uppajjanakaanuvādavasena vuttaṃ. Āpatti nidānaṃ assāti āpattinidānaṃ. Āpattādhikaraṇapaccayā catasso āpattiyo āpajjatīti evaṃ āpattiṃ nissāya uppajjanakaāpattivasenetaṃ vuttaṃ. Kiccayaṃ nidānamassāti kiccayanidānaṃ; catubbidhaṃ saṅghakammaṃ kāraṇamassāti attho. Ukkhittānuvattikāya bhikkhuniyā yāvatatiyaṃ samanubhāsanādīnaṃ kiccaṃ nissāya uppajjanakakiccānaṃ vasenetaṃ vuttaṃ. Ayaṃ catunnampi adhikaraṇānaṃ vissajjanapakkhe ekapadayojanā. Etenānusārena sabbapadāni yojetabbāni.

In 'source-of-dispute' (vivādanidāna) and so on: 'dispute' (vivāda), which is reckoned as the eighteen grounds for contention, is its source, hence 'source-of-dispute'. This is stated in the sense of a future dispute arising dependent on a previous dispute. 'Reproach' (anuvāda) is its source, hence 'source-of-reproach' (anuvādanidāna). This too is stated in the sense of a future reproach arising dependent on a previous reproach. 'Offense' (āpatti) is its source, hence 'source-of-offense' (āpattinidāna). Since, with a legal issue concerning an offense as the condition, one falls into the four kinds of offenses, this is stated in the sense of a future offense arising dependent on a previous offense. 'Duty' (kiccaya) is its source, hence 'source-of-duty' (kiccayanidāna); the meaning is that the fourfold Saṅgha-act is its cause. This is stated in the sense of future duties arising dependent on the duty of admonishing up to the third time, etc., a nun who follows a suspended monk. This is the application of a single word in the section of answering for all four legal issues. According to this method, all the words should be connected.

Dutiyapucchāya [M 0.0198] hetunidānantiādimhi vissajjane navannaṃ kusalākusalābyākatahetūnaṃ vasena hetunidānāditā veditabbā. Tatiyapucchāya vissajjane byañjanamattaṃ nānaṃ. Hetuyeva hi ettha paccayoti vutto.

In the answer to the second question, regarding 'cause-sourced' (hetunidāna) and so on, the state of being 'cause-sourced', etc., should be understood by way of the nine causes—wholesome, unwholesome, and indeterminate. In the answer to the third question, there is merely a difference in wording. For here, cause itself is said to be 'condition'.

343. Mūlapucchāya vissajjane dvādasa mūlānīti kodhaupanāhayugaḷakādīni cha vivādāmūlāni, lobhadosamohā tayo, alobhādosāmohā tayoti imāni ajjhattasantānappavattāni dvādasa mūlāni. Cuddasa mūlānīti tāneva dvādasa [P 7.1358] kāyavācāhi saddhiṃ cuddasa honti. Cha mūlānīti kāyādīni cha samuṭṭhānāni.

343. In the answer to the question on roots: 'twelve roots' means the six roots of disputes, such as the pair of anger and hostility; the three which are greed, hatred, and delusion; and the three which are non-greed, non-hatred, and non-delusion. These are the twelve roots that arise within the internal continuum. 'Fourteen roots' means that those same twelve, together with body and speech, become fourteen. 'Six roots' means the six origins, such as the body.

Samuṭṭhānapucchāya vissajjane aṭṭhārasa bhedakaravatthūni samuṭṭhānāni, tañhi etesu aṭṭhārasasu bhedakaravatthūsu samuṭṭhāti, etehi vā kāraṇabhūtehi samuṭṭhāti. Tenassetāni samuṭṭhānāni vuccanti. Esa nayo sabbattha.

In the answer to the question on origins, the eighteen grounds for creating division are the origins. For that legal question concerning disputes arises in these eighteen grounds for creating division, or it arises due to these which have become causes. Therefore, these are called its origins. This is the method in all cases.

344. Vivādādhikaraṇaṃ āpattītiādibhede ekena adhikaraṇena kiccādhikaraṇenāti idaṃ yena adhikaraṇena sammanti, taṃ dassetuṃ vuttaṃ, na panetāni ekaṃsato kiccādhikaraṇeneva sammanti. Na hi puggalassa santike desentassa kiccādhikaraṇaṃ nāma atthi.

344. In the classification of legal questions concerning disputes, offenses, and so on, the statement 'by one legal question, by the legal question concerning duties' was said to show by which legal question they are settled. However, these legal questions are not settled exclusively by the legal question concerning duties alone. For indeed, for a monk confessing in the presence of an individual, there is no such thing as a legal question concerning duties.

Na katamena samathenāti sāvasesāpatti viya na sammati. Na hi sakkā sā desetuṃ, na tato vuṭṭhāya suddhante patiṭṭhātuṃ.

Regarding 'not by any settlement': like an offense with a remainder, it is not settled. For indeed, it is not possible to confess it, nor is it possible to rise from it and be established in a state of purity.

348. Vivādādhikaraṇaṃ hoti anuvādādhikaraṇantiādi nayo uttānoyeva.

348. The method beginning with 'a legal question concerning disputes becomes a legal question concerning reproaches' and so on, is simply clear.

349. Tato [V 0.0200] paraṃ yattha sativinayotiādikā sammukhāvinayaṃ amuñcitvā cha yamakapucchā vuttā, tāsaṃ vissajjaneneva attho pakāsito.

349. After that, the six paired questions beginning with 'where there is a settlement by mindfulness' were stated without abandoning the settlement in the presence of both parties. The meaning of these is made clear by their very answers.

351. Saṃsaṭṭhādipucchānaṃ vissajjane saṃsaṭṭhāti sativinayakammavācākkhaṇasmiṃyeva dvinnampi samathānaṃ siddhattā sammukhāvinayoti vā sativinayoti vā ime dhammā saṃsaṭṭhā, no visaṃsaṭṭhā. Yasmā pana kadalikkhandhe pattavaṭṭīnaṃ viya na sakkā tesaṃ vinibbhujitvā nānākaraṇaṃ dassetuṃ, tena vuttaṃ [M 0.0199] “na ca labbhā imesaṃ dhammānaṃ vinibbhujitvā vinibbhujitvā nānākaraṇaṃ paññāpetu”nti. Esa nayo sabbattha.

351. In the answer to the questions beginning with 'connected': 'connected' means that since both settlements—namely, settlement in the presence of both parties and settlement by mindfulness—are accomplished at the very moment of the formal act of settlement by mindfulness, these principles are connected, not disconnected. However, because it is not possible to separate these principles and demonstrate their distinctness in the way that one can separate the sheaths of a banana trunk, it was therefore said, 'And it is not possible to make known a distinction for these principles by separating them again and again.' This is the method in all cases.

Sattasamathanidānavaṇṇanā

The Explanation of the Origin of the Seven Settlements

352. Kiṃnidānoti pucchāvissajjane nidānaṃ nidānamassāti nidānanidāno. Tattha saṅghasammukhatā, dhammasammukhatā, vinayasammukhatā, puggalasammukhatāti idaṃ sammukhāvinayassa nidānaṃ. Sativepullapatto khīṇāsavo laddhupavādo sativinayassa nidānaṃ. Ummattako bhikkhu amūḷhavinayassa nidānaṃ. Yo ca deseti, yassa ca deseti, ubhinnaṃ sammukhībhāvo paṭiññātakaraṇassa nidānaṃ. Bhaṇḍanajātānaṃ adhikaraṇaṃ vūpasametuṃ asakkuṇeyyatā yebhuyyasikāya nidānaṃ. Pāpussanno puggalo tassapāpiyasikāya nidānaṃ. Bhaṇḍanajātānaṃ bahu assāmaṇakaajjhācāro tiṇavatthārakassa nidānaṃ. Hetupaccayavārā vuttanayā eva.

352. In the answer to the question 'What is its origin?': It is called 'caused-by-an-origin' because it has a cause which is its origin. Therein, the presence of the Sangha, the presence of the Dhamma, the presence of the Vinaya, and the presence of the individual—this is the origin of the settlement in the presence of both parties. An Arahant who has attained fullness of mindfulness and has received an accusation is the origin of the settlement by mindfulness. An insane monk is the origin of the settlement for one who is not out of his mind. The presence of both the one who confesses and the one to whom he confesses is the origin of the settlement by confession. The inability to settle a legal question that has arisen among those who are quarreling is the origin of the settlement by majority vote. A person with much evil is the origin of the settlement for one's further depravity. The many unmonastic transgressions of those who are quarreling is the origin of the settlement of covering over with grass. The sections on cause and condition are just as has been stated.

353. Mūlapucchāya vissajjanaṃ uttānameva. Samuṭṭhānapucchāya kiñcāpi [P 7.1359] “sattannaṃ samathānaṃ katame chattiṃsa samuṭṭhānā”ti vuttaṃ, sammukhāvinayassa pana kammasaṅgahābhāvena samuṭṭhānābhāvato channaṃyeva samathānaṃ cha samuṭṭhānāni vibhattāni. Tattha kammassa kiriyāti ñatti veditabbā. Karaṇanti tassāyeva ñattiyā ṭhapetabbakāle ṭhapanaṃ. Upagamananti sayaṃ upagamanaṃ; attanāyeva tassa kammassa karaṇanti attho. Ajjhupagamananti ajjhesanupagamanaṃ; aññaṃ saddhivihārikādikaṃ “idaṃ kammaṃ karohī”ti ajjhesananti attho. Adhivāsanāti “ruccati me etaṃ, karotu saṅgho”ti evaṃ adhivāsanā; chandadānanti attho. Appaṭikkosanāti “na metaṃ khamati, mā evaṃ karothā”ti appaṭisedhanā. Iti channaṃ chakkānaṃ vasena chattiṃsa samuṭṭhānā veditabbā.

353. The answer to the root question is straightforward. In the question on origins, although it was said, “Of the seven settlements, what are the thirty-six origins?”, because the settlement in the presence of both parties is not included in a formal act and thus has no origins, the six origins for only six of the settlements have been analyzed. Therein, by 'performance of the act' (kammassa kiriyā), the motion (ñatti) should be understood. By 'execution' (karaṇa), the placing of that very motion at the time it should be placed should be understood. By 'approach' (upagamana), approaching oneself should be understood; the meaning is performing that act by oneself. By 'approaching by request' (ajjhupagamana), approaching through exhortation should be understood; the meaning is exhorting another, such as a co-resident, saying, “Perform this act.” By 'assent' (adhivāsanā), assenting thus, “This is agreeable to me, let the Sangha do it,” should be understood; the meaning is giving consent (chanda). By 'non-rejection' (appaṭikkosanā), not forbidding thus, “This is not agreeable to me, do not do this,” should be understood. Thus, by way of six groups of six, the thirty-six origins should be understood.

Sattasamathanānātthādivaṇṇanā

Explanation of the Various Meanings, etc., of the Seven Settlements

354. Nānātthapucchāvissajjanaṃ [V 0.0201] uttānameva. Adhikaraṇapucchāvissajjane ayaṃ vivādo no adhikaraṇanti ayaṃ mātāputtādīnaṃ vivādo viruddhavādattā vivādo nāma hoti, samathehi pana adhikaraṇīyatāya abhāvato adhikaraṇaṃ na hoti. Anuvādādīsupi eseva nayo. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānamevāti.

354. The answer to the question on various meanings is straightforward. In the answer to the question on legal questions, this—‘this is a dispute, not a legal question’—is called a dispute because it involves conflicting statements, such as between mother and son. However, since it lacks the quality of being a matter to be settled by the formal procedures, it is not a legal question. The same method applies to reproaches and the like. The rest is straightforward in all instances.

Adhikaraṇabhedavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Explanation of the Classifications of Legal Questions is Concluded.

Dutiyagāthāsaṅgaṇikaṃ

Section on the Second Verse

Codanādipucchāvissajjanāvaṇṇanā

Explanation of the Questions and Answers regarding Accusation, etc.

359. Dutiyagāthāsaṅgaṇiyaṃ [M 0.0200][V 0.0202] codanāti vatthuñca āpattiñca dassetvā codanā. Sāraṇāti dosasāraṇā. Saṅgho kimatthāyāti saṅghasannipāto kimatthāya. Matikammaṃ pana kissa kāraṇāti matikammaṃ vuccati mantaggahaṇaṃ; taṃ kissa kāraṇāti attho.

359. In the Section on the Second Verse, “accusation” is the pointing out of the matter and the offense. “Reminder” is reminding of the fault. “For what purpose is the Sangha?” means for what purpose is the assembly of the Sangha. In “But for what reason is the deliberation?,” “deliberation” refers to the taking of counsel; the meaning is, for what reason is that taking of counsel done?

Codanā sāraṇatthāyāti vuttappakārā codanā, tena cuditakapuggalena codakadosasāraṇatthāya. Niggahatthāya sāraṇāti dosasāraṇā pana tassa puggalassa niggahatthāya. Saṅgho pariggahatthāyāti tattha sannipatito saṅgho vinicchayapariggahaṇatthāya; dhammādhammaṃ tulanatthāya suvinicchitadubbinicchitaṃ jānanatthāyāti attho. Matikammaṃ pana pāṭiyekkanti suttantikattherānañca vinayadharattherānañca mantaggahaṇaṃ [P 7.1360] pāṭekkaṃ pāṭekkaṃ vinicchayasanniṭṭhāpanatthaṃ.

“An accusation is for the purpose of reminding”—the accusation of the type mentioned is for the purpose of reminding the accused person of the fault. “A reminder is for the purpose of restraint”—the reminder of the fault, however, is for the purpose of restraining that person. “The Sangha is for the purpose of grasping”—here, the assembled Sangha is for the purpose of grasping the decision; the meaning is for the purpose of weighing what is Dhamma and what is not Dhamma, and for the purpose of knowing what is well-decided and what is poorly-decided. “But the deliberation is individual”—the taking of counsel by the Suttanta elders and the Vinaya elders is done individually, each one, for the purpose of bringing the decision to a conclusion.

Mā kho paṭighanti cuditake vā codake vā kopaṃ mā janayi. Sace anuvijjako tuvanti sace tvaṃ saṅghamajjhe otiṇṇaṃ adhikaraṇaṃ vinicchituṃ nisinno vinayadharo.

“Do not be resentful”—do not generate anger in the accused or the accuser. “If you are an investigator”—if you are a Vinaya-holder seated in the midst of the Sangha to decide a formal case that has arisen.

Viggāhikanti “na tvaṃ imaṃ dhammavinayaṃ ājānāsī”tiādinayappavattaṃ. Anatthasaṃhitanti yā anatthaṃ janayati, parisaṃ khobhetvā uṭṭhāpeti, evarūpiṃ kathaṃ mā abhaṇi. Sutte vinaye vātiādīsu suttaṃ nāma ubhatovibhaṅgo. Vinayo nāma khandhako. Anulomo nāma parivāro. Paññattaṃ nāma sakalaṃ vinayapiṭakaṃ. Anulomikaṃ nāma cattāro mahāpadesā.

“Contentious” means that which proceeds in the manner of “you do not understand this Dhamma and Vinaya,” and so forth. “Unconnected with benefit” means talk that produces what is not beneficial, that agitates the assembly and causes them to get up and leave—do not speak such talk. In “in the Sutta or Vinaya,” etc., Sutta means the Ubhatovibhaṅga. Vinaya means the Khandhakas. Anuloma means the Parivāra. Paññatta means the entire Vinayapiṭaka. Anulomika means the four Mahāpadesas.

Anuyogavattaṃ [V 0.0203] nisāmayāti anuyuñjanavattaṃ nisāmehi. Kusalena buddhimatā katanti chekena paṇḍitena ñāṇapāramippattena bhagavatā nīharitvā ṭhapitaṃ. Suvuttanti supaññāpitaṃ. Sikkhāpadānulomikanti sikkhāpadānaṃ anulomaṃ. Ayaṃ tāva padattho, ayaṃ panettha sādhippāyasaṅkhepavaṇṇanā – “sace tvaṃ anuvijjako, mā sahasā bhaṇi, mā anatthasaṃhitaṃ viggāhikakathaṃ [M 0.0201] bhaṇi. Yaṃ pana kusalena buddhimatā lokanāthena etesu suttādīsu anuyogavattaṃ kathaṃ supaññattaṃ sabbasikkhāpadānaṃ anulomaṃ, taṃ nisāmaya taṃ upadhārehī”ti. Gatiṃ na nāsento samparāyikanti attano samparāye sugatinibbattiṃ anāsento anuyogavattaṃ nisāmaya. Yo hi taṃ anisāmetvā anuyuñjati, so samparāyikaṃ attano gatiṃ nāseti, tasmā tvaṃ anāsento nisāmayāti attho. Idāni taṃ anuyogavattaṃ dassetuṃ hitesītiādimāha. Tattha hitesīti hitaṃ esanto gavesanto; mettañca mettāpubbabhāgañca upaṭṭhapetvāti attho. Kālenāti yuttapattakālena; ajjhesitakāleyeva tava bhāre kate anuyuñjāti attho.

“Observe the practice of inquiry” means: observe the procedure of inquiry. “Made by the skillful and wise” means: brought forth and established by the Blessed One, who is skilled, truly wise, and has reached the perfection of knowledge. “Well-spoken” means: well-proclaimed. “In accordance with the training rules” means: conforming to the training rules. This, firstly, is the meaning of the words. Herein, this is the concise explanation with the intended meaning: “If you are an investigator, do not speak hastily; do not speak contentious talk that is unconnected with benefit. But as for the practice of inquiry in these Suttas, etc., which was made and well-proclaimed by the skillful, wise Lord of the World to be in conformity with all the training rules—observe that, reflect on that.” “Not destroying one’s future destiny” means: not destroying rebirth in a good destination in one’s future life, observe the practice of inquiry. For whoever inquires without observing it destroys their own future destiny; therefore, the meaning is, you should observe it without destroying your destiny. Now, to show that practice of inquiry, the Venerable Upāli said, “desiring welfare,” etc. Therein, “desiring welfare” means: seeking and searching for what is beneficial; the meaning is, having established both loving-kindness and the preliminary stage of loving-kindness. “At the proper time” means: at a suitable and appropriate time; the meaning is, you should inquire only at the time you are requested, when the responsibility has been placed upon you.

Sahasā vohāraṃ mā padhāresīti yo etesaṃ sahasā vohāro hoti, sahasā bhāsitaṃ, taṃ mā padhāresi, mā gaṇhittha.

“Do not hastily accept the statement” means: whatever hasty statement is made by these, what is spoken hastily—do not take note of that statement, do not take it as conclusive.

Paṭiññānusandhitena kārayeti ettha anusandhitanti kathānusandhi vuccati, tasmā paṭiññānusandhinā kāraye; kathānusandhiṃ sallakkhetvā paṭiññāya kārayeti attho. Atha vā paṭiññāya ca anusandhitena ca kāraye, lajjiṃ paṭiññāya kāraye; alajjiṃ vattānusandhināti attho. Tasmā eva [P 7.1361] paṭiññā lajjīsūti gāthamāha. Tattha vattānusandhitena kārayeti vattānusandhinā kāraye, yā assa vattena saddhiṃ paṭiññā sandhiyati, tāya paṭiññāya kārayeti attho.

In the phrase, “One should have him proceed by means of acknowledgement and connection,” herein, “connection” refers to the coherence of the testimony. Therefore, one should have him proceed by means of the coherence of the testimony that is his acknowledgement; the meaning is that, having noted the coherence of the testimony, one should have him proceed by means of acknowledgement. Alternatively, one should have him proceed by means of acknowledgement and connection: one should have a modest person proceed by means of acknowledgement, and one should have a shameless person proceed by means of the coherence of his conduct. This is the meaning. Therefore, the Venerable Upāli spoke the verse beginning, “Acknowledgement for the modest.” Therein, “one should have him proceed by means of the coherence of his conduct” means one should have him proceed by means of the coherence of his conduct; the meaning is, one should have him proceed by means of that acknowledgement which is coherent with his conduct.

Sañciccāti jānanto āpajjati. Parigūhatīti nigūhati na deseti na vuṭṭhāti.

“Knowingly” means: knowing, one commits an offense. “Conceals” means: one hides it, does not confess it, and does not emerge from it.

Sā ahampi jānāmīti yaṃ tumhehi vuttaṃ, taṃ saccaṃ, ahampi naṃ evameva jānāmi. Aññañca tāhanti aññañca taṃ ahaṃ pucchāmi.

“That I also know” means: what was said by you is true; I too know it in just that way. “And another I will ask you” means: I will ask you another question.

Pubbāparaṃ [V 0.0204] na jānātīti purekathitañca pacchākathitañca na jānāti. Akovidoti tasmiṃ pubbāpare akusalo. Anusandhivacanapathaṃ na jānātīti kathānusandhivacanaṃ vinicchayānusandhivacanañca na jānāti.

“Does not know the sequence” means: one does not understand what was said before and what was said after. “Is not an expert” means: one is unskilled in that sequence of what was said before and after. “Does not know the path of coherent speech” means: one does not know the coherent speech of the testimony nor the coherent speech of the legal decision.

Sīlavipattiyā codetīti dvīhi āpattikkhandhehi codeti. Ācāradiṭṭhiyāti ācāravipattiyā ceva diṭṭhivipattiyā ca. Ācāravipattiyā codento pañcahāpattikkhandhehi codeti, diṭṭhivipattiyā codento [M 0.0202] micchādiṭṭhiyā ceva antaggāhikadiṭṭhiyā ca codeti. Ājīvenapi codetīti ājīvahetupaññattehi chahi sikkhāpadehi codeti. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānamevāti.

“One accuses of a failure in virtue” means: one accuses by means of the two groups of offenses. “By conduct and view” means: by a failure in conduct and a failure in view. One who accuses of a failure in conduct accuses by means of the five groups of offenses. One who accuses of a failure in view accuses by means of wrong view and by means of a view that grasps at extremes. “One also accuses regarding livelihood” means: one accuses by means of the six training rules laid down on account of livelihood. The remainder is clear everywhere.

Dutiyagāthāsaṅgaṇikavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Explanation of the Section on the Second Verse is concluded.

Codanākaṇḍaṃ

The Section on Accusation

Anuvijjakakiccavaṇṇanā

The Exposition on the Investigator's Duty

360-361. Idāni [M 0.0203][V 0.0205] evaṃ uppannāya codanāya vinayadharena kattabbakiccaṃ dassetuṃ anuvijjakenātiādi āraddhaṃ. Tattha diṭṭhaṃ diṭṭhenāti gāthāya ayamattho – ekeneko mātugāmena saddhiṃ ekaṭṭhānato nikkhamanto vā pavisanto vā diṭṭho, so taṃ pārājikena codeti, itaro tassa dassanaṃ anujānāti. Taṃ pana dassanaṃ paṭicca pārājikaṃ na upeti, na paṭijānāti. Evamettha yaṃ tena diṭṭhaṃ, taṃ tassa “diṭṭho mayā”ti iminā diṭṭhavacanena sameti. Yasmā pana itaro taṃ dassanaṃ paṭicca dosaṃ na paṭijānāti, tasmā asuddhaparisaṅkito hoti; amūlakaparisaṅkitoti attho. Tassa puggalassa “suddho aha”nti paṭiññāya tena saddhiṃ uposatho kātabbo. Sesagāthādvayepi eseva nayo. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānamevāti.

360-361. Now, to show the duty to be performed by a Vinaya-holder regarding an accusation that has arisen in this way, the Elder Upāli began with the phrase 'by the investigator' and so on. Herein, this is the meaning of the verse beginning 'What is seen, by the seen': One monk is seen by another monk coming out from or entering into the same place together with a woman. The former accuses the latter of a pārājika offense, while the latter admits to the seeing. However, based on that seeing, he does not approach a pārājika offense, nor does he confess to it. Thus, herein, what was seen by the former is consistent with his statement, 'He was seen by me.' But because the latter does not confess to a fault based on that seeing, he becomes one suspected of impurity; the meaning is, he becomes one suspected without basis. Because of that person's declaration, 'I am pure,' the Uposatha should be performed with him. In the remaining two verses also, this is the same method. The rest is clear throughout.

Anuvijjakakiccavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Exposition on the Investigator's Duty is concluded.

Codakapucchāvissajjanāvaṇṇanā

The Exposition on the Answers to the Accuser's Questions

362-363. Codanāya [P 7.1362] ko ādītiādipucchānaṃ vissajjane sacce ca akuppe cāti sacce patiṭṭhātabbaṃ akuppe ca. Yaṃ kataṃ vā na kataṃ vā, tadeva vattabbaṃ, na ca codake vā anuvijjake vā saṅghe vā kopo uppādetabbo. Otiṇṇānotiṇṇaṃ jānitabbanti otiṇṇañca anotiṇṇañca vacanaṃ jānitabbaṃ. Tatrāyaṃ jānanavidhi – ettakā codakassa pubbakathā, ettakā pacchimakathā, ettakā cuditakassa pubbakathā, ettakā pacchimakathāti jānitabbā[V 0.0206]. Codakassa pamāṇaṃ gaṇhitabbaṃ, cuditakassa pamāṇaṃ gaṇhitabbaṃ, anuvijjakassa pamāṇaṃ gaṇhitabbaṃ, anuvijjako appamattakampi ahāpento “āvuso samannāharitvā ujuṃ katvā āharā”ti vattabbo, saṅghena evaṃ paṭipajjitabbaṃ. Yena dhammena yena vinayena yena satthusāsanena taṃ adhikaraṇaṃ vūpasammatīti ettha dhammoti bhūtaṃ vatthu. Vinayoti codanā ceva sāraṇā ca. Satthusāsananti ñattisampadā ceva anussāvanasampadā [M 0.0204] ca. Etena hi dhammena ca vinayena ca satthusāsanena ca adhikaraṇaṃ vūpasamati, tasmā anuvijjakena bhūtena vatthunā codetvā āpattiṃ sāretvā ñattisampadāya ceva anussāvanasampadāya ca taṃ adhikaraṇaṃ vūpasametabbaṃ, anuvijjakena evaṃ paṭipajjitabbaṃ. Sesamettha uttānameva.

362-363. In the answer to questions beginning with 'What is the beginning of an accusation?', the accused should be established in truth and in non-anger. What has been done or what has not been done, that very thing should be stated, and anger should not be produced towards the accuser, the investigator, or the Saṅgha. 'One should know what has entered and what has not entered' means one should know the statement that has entered and the statement that has not entered. Therein, this is the method of knowing: the accuser’s prior statement is of such an extent, the later statement is of such an extent; the accused’s prior statement is of such an extent, the later statement is of such an extent—thus it should be known. The measure of the accuser should be taken, the measure of the accused should be taken, the measure of the investigator should be taken. The investigator, not omitting even a little, should be told, 'Venerable sir, having paid close attention and made it straight, present it.' The Saṅgha should practice in this way. 'By which Dhamma, by which Vinaya, by which Teacher’s instruction that legal issue is settled'—herein, 'Dhamma' means the actual fact. 'Vinaya' means the accusation and the reminding. 'Teacher’s instruction' means the accomplishment of the motion and the accomplishment of the proclamation. Indeed, it is by this Dhamma, this Vinaya, and this Teacher’s instruction that the legal issue is settled. Therefore, the investigator, having accused with the actual fact and having caused the offense to be remembered, should settle that legal issue by means of the accomplishment of the motion and the accomplishment of the proclamation. The investigator should practice in this way. The rest here is clear indeed.

364. Uposatho kimatthāyātiādipucchāvissajjanampi uttānameva. Avasānagāthāsu there ca paribhāsatīti avamaññaṃ karonto “kiṃ ime jānantī”ti paribhāsati. Khato upahatindriyoti tāya chandādigāmitāya tena ca paribhāsanena attanā attano khatattā khato. Saddhādīnañca indriyānaṃ upahatattā upahatindriyo. Nirayaṃ gacchati dummedho, na ca sikkhāya gāravoti so khato upahatindriyo paññāya abhāvato dummedho tīsu sikkhāsu asikkhanato na ca sikkhāya gāravo kāyassa bhedā nirayameva upagacchati, tasmā na ca āmisaṃ nissāya…pe… yathā dhammo tathā kareti. Tassattho na ca āmisaṃ nissāya kare, cuditakacodakesu hi aññatarena dinnaṃ cīvarādiāmisaṃ gaṇhanto āmisaṃ nissāya karoti, evaṃ na kareyya. Na ca nissāya puggalanti “ayaṃ me upajjhāyo vā ācariyo vā”tiādinā nayena chandādīhi gacchanto puggalaṃ nissāya karoti, evaṃ na kareyya. Atha kho ubhopete vivajjetvā yathā dhammo ṭhito, tatheva kareyyāti.

364. The answer to the questions beginning with 'For what purpose is the Uposatha?' is also clear indeed. In the concluding verses, 'and he scolds the elders' means: acting with contempt, he scolds them, saying, 'What do these elders know?'. 'Ruined, with destroyed faculties' means: by that going into bias, etc., and by that scolding, because he has ruined himself by himself, he is 'ruined'. And because his faculties beginning with faith have been destroyed, he is 'one with destroyed faculties'. 'The one of little wisdom goes to hell, and has no reverence for the training' means: that person who is ruined, with destroyed faculties, being of little wisdom due to the absence of wisdom, and having no reverence for the training because of not training in the three trainings, at the breaking up of the body, approaches hell indeed. Therefore, it was said by the venerable Elder Upāli: 'And not relying on material gain... one should act as the Dhamma is.' The meaning of this is: one should not act relying on material gain. For one who accepts robes or other material things given by either the accused or the accuser acts relying on material gain; one should not act in this way. 'And not relying on a person' means: one who proceeds with bias, etc., by the method beginning with 'This is my preceptor or my teacher,' acts relying on a person; one should not act in this way. Rather, having avoided both of these, just as the Dhamma stands, so should one act.

Upakaṇṇakaṃ jappatīti [P 7.1363] “evaṃ kathehi, mā evaṃ kathayitthā”ti kaṇṇamūle manteti. Jimhaṃ pekkhatīti dosameva gavesati. Vītiharatīti vinicchayaṃ hāpeti. Kummaggaṃ paṭisevatīti āpattiṃ dīpeti.

'He whispers into the ear' means: he consults at the base of the ear, saying, "Speak like this, do not speak like that." 'He looks askance' means: he seeks only for a fault. 'He causes deviation' means: he causes a judgment to be lost. 'He follows a wrong path' means: he points out an offense.

Akālena ca codetīti anokāse anajjhiṭṭhova codeti. Pubbāparaṃ na jānātīti purimakathañca pacchimakathañca na jānāti.

'And he accuses at an improper time' means: he accuses at an improper occasion, being uninvited. 'He does not know the prior and the subsequent' means: he does not know the prior discourse and the subsequent discourse.

Anusandhivacanapathaṃ [V 0.0207] na jānātīti kathānusandhivinicchayānusandhivasena vacanaṃ na jānāti. Sesaṃ sabbattha uttānamevāti.

'He does not know the path of connected speech' means: he does not know the speech by way of the connection of the narrative and the connection of the judgment. The rest is clear throughout.

Codanākaṇḍavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Exposition on the Section on Accusation is concluded.

Cūḷasaṅgāmo

The Minor Battle

Anuvijjakassa paṭipattivaṇṇanā

An Explanation of the Practice of an Investigator

365. Cūḷasaṅgāme [M 0.0205][V 0.0208] saṅgāmāvacarena bhikkhunāti saṅgāmo vuccati adhikaraṇavinicchayatthāya saṅghasannipāto. Tatra hi attapaccatthikā ceva sāsanapaccatthikā ca uddhammaṃ ubbinayaṃ satthu sāsanaṃ dīpentā samosaranti vesālikā vajjiputtakā viya. Yo bhikkhu tesaṃ paccatthikānaṃ laddhiṃ madditvā sakavādadīpanatthāya tattha avacarati, ajjhogāhetvā vinicchayaṃ pavatteti, so saṅgāmāvacaro nāma yasatthero viya. Tena saṅgāmāvacarena bhikkhunā saṅghaṃ upasaṅkamantena nīcacittena saṅgho upasaṅkamitabbo. Nīcacittenāti mānaddhajaṃ nipātetvā nihatamānacittena. Rajoharaṇasamenāti pādapuñchanasamena; yathā rajoharaṇassa saṃkiliṭṭhe vā asaṃkiliṭṭhe vā pāde puñchiyamāne neva rāgo na doso; evaṃ iṭṭhāniṭṭhesu arajjantena adussantenāti attho. Yathāpatirūpe āsaneti yathāpatirūpaṃ āsanaṃ ñatvā attano pāpuṇanaṭṭhāne therānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ piṭṭhiṃ adassetvā nisīditabbaṃ. Anānākathikenāti nānāvidhaṃ taṃ taṃ anatthakathaṃ akathentena. Atiracchānakathikenāti diṭṭhasutamutampi rājakathādikaṃ tiracchānakathaṃ akathentena. Sāmaṃ vā dhammo bhāsitabboti saṅghasannipātaṭṭhāne kappiyākappiyanissitā vā rūpārūpaparicchedasamathācāravipassanācāraṭṭhānanisajjavattādinissitā vā kathā dhammo nāma. Evarūpo dhammo sayaṃ vā bhāsitabbo, paro vā ajjhesitabbo. Yo bhikkhu tathārūpiṃ kathaṃ kathetuṃ pahoti, so vattabbo – “āvuso saṅghamajjhamhi [P 7.1364] pañhe uppanne tvaṃ katheyyāsī”ti. Ariyo vā tuṇhībhāvo nātimaññitabboti ariyā tuṇhī nisīdantā na bālaputhujjanā viya nisīdanti. Aññataraṃ kammaṭṭhānaṃ gahetvāva nisīdanti. Iti kammaṭṭhānamanasikāravasena tuṇhībhāvo [V 0.0209] ariyo tuṇhībhāvo nāma, so nātimaññitabbo, kiṃ kammaṭṭhānānuyogenāti nāvajānitabbo; attano patirūpaṃ kammaṭṭhānaṃ gahetvāva nisīditabbanti attho.

365. In the Cūḷasaṅgāma, regarding 'by a monk who frequents the battleground': an assembly of the Saṅgha for the purpose of deciding a legal issue is called a 'battle'. For in that assembly, both personal adversaries and adversaries of the Dispensation gather, presenting the Teacher's Dispensation as non-Dhamma and non-Vinaya, like the Vajjiputtaka monks of Vesālī. A monk who, having crushed the views of those adversaries, frequents that assembly to clarify his own doctrine, and having entered into it, brings about a decision, is called a 'battle-goer', like the Elder Yasa. When such a battle-goer monk approaches the Saṅgha, he should approach the Saṅgha with a humble mind. 'With a humble mind' means having felled the banner of pride, with a mind of humbled pride. 'Like a dust-remover' means like a foot-wiping cloth; just as for a dust-remover, when a soiled or unsoiled foot is being wiped, there is neither lust nor hatred, so too, one should be without attachment and aversion towards desirable and undesirable objects—this is the meaning. 'In a suitable seat' means that having known a suitable seat, one should sit in the place one has reached without showing one's back to the elder monks. 'Not by one who speaks various kinds of talk' means not speaking various kinds of useless talk. 'Not by one who speaks animal-talk' means not speaking animal-talk, such as talk about kings, etc., even if it is based on what is seen, heard, or cognized. 'Or the Dhamma should be spoken by oneself' means that in the assembly of the Saṅgha, talk based on what is allowable and unallowable, or based on the distinction of materiality and mentality, the practice of serenity, the practice of insight, standing, sitting, the duties of the Khandhakas, and so on, is called Dhamma. One should either speak such Dhamma oneself, or request another to speak. A monk who is able to speak such talk should be requested: 'Friend, when a question has arisen in the midst of the Saṅgha, you should speak.' 'Or noble silence should not be despised' means that when noble ones sit in silence, they do not sit like foolish worldlings. They sit only after having taken up a meditation subject. Thus, silence by way of attending to a meditation subject is called 'noble silence'. It should not be despised, nor should it be disdained by thinking, 'What is the use of applying oneself to a meditation subject?' The meaning is: one should sit only after having taken up a meditation subject suitable for oneself.

Na upajjhāyo pucchitabboti “ko nāmo tuyhaṃ upajjhāyo”ti na pucchitabbo. Esa nayo sabbattha. Na jātīti “khattiyajātiyo tvaṃ brāhmaṇajātiyo”ti [M 0.0206] evaṃ jāti na pucchitabbā. Na āgamoti “dīghabhāṇakosi tvaṃ majjhimabhāṇako”ti evaṃ āgamo na pucchitabbo. Kulapadesoti khattiyakulādivaseneva veditabbo. Atrassa pemaṃ vā doso vāti atra puggale etesaṃ kāraṇānaṃ aññataravasena pemaṃ vā bhaveyya doso vā.

'The preceptor should not be asked' means one should not ask, 'What is the name of your preceptor?' This is the method in all cases. 'Not the birth' means the birth should not be asked, thus: 'Are you of the khattiya birth or the brāhmaṇa birth?' 'Not the textual tradition' means the textual tradition should not be asked, thus: 'Are you a reciter of the Dīgha Nikāya or a reciter of the Majjhima Nikāya?' 'The indication of family' should be understood by way of the khattiya family and so on. 'Towards this one there might be affection or aversion' means that towards this person, on account of one or another of these reasons, there might be affection or there might be aversion.

No parisakappikenāti parisakappakena parisānuvidhāyakena na bhavitabbaṃ; yaṃ parisāya ruccati, tadeva cetetvā kappetvā na kathetabbanti attho. Na hatthamuddā dassetabbāti kathetabbe ca akathetabbe ca saññājananatthaṃ hatthavikāro na kātabbo.

'Not by one who schemes for the assembly' means one should not be a schemer for the assembly or one who acts in accordance with the assembly. The meaning is that one should not speak only what is agreeable to the assembly, having intended and contrived it. 'Hand gestures should not be shown' means that whether in what should be said or what should not be said, an unusual movement of the hand should not be made for the purpose of giving a sign.

Atthaṃ anuvidhiyantenāti vinicchayapaṭivedhameva sallakkhentena “idaṃ suttaṃ upalabbhati, imasmiṃ vinicchaye idaṃ vakkhāmī”ti evaṃ paritulayantena nisīditabbanti attho. Na ca āsanā vuṭṭhātabbanti na āsanā vuṭṭhāya sannipātamaṇḍale vicaritabbaṃ, vinayadhare uṭṭhite sabbā parisā uṭṭhahati. Na vītihātabbanti na vinicchayo hāpetabbo. Na kummaggo sevitabboti na āpatti dīpetabbā. Asāhasikena bhavitabbanti na sahasākārinā bhavitabbaṃ; sahasā duruttavacanaṃ na kathetabbanti attho. Vacanakkhamenāti duruttavācaṃ khamanasīlena. Hitaparisakkināti hitesinā hitagavesinā karuṇā ca karuṇāpubbabhāgo ca upaṭṭhāpetabboti ayaṃ padadvayepi adhippāyo. Anasuruttenāti na asuruttena. Asuruttaṃ vuccati viggāhikakathāsaṅkhātaṃ asundaravacanaṃ; taṃ na kathetabbanti attho. Attā pariggahetabboti “vinicchinituṃ vūpasametuṃ sakkhissāmi nu kho no”ti evaṃ attā pariggahetabbo; attano pamāṇaṃ jānitabbanti attho. Paro pariggahetabboti “lajjiyā nu kho ayaṃ parisā, sakkā saññāpetuṃ [P 7.1365] udāhu no”ti evaṃ paro pariggahetabbo.

'By one who conforms to the meaning' means that one should sit, marking only the penetration of the decision, weighing and considering thus: 'This Sutta is available; in this decision I will say this.' This is the meaning. 'And one should not rise from the seat' means one should not rise from one's seat and wander about in the assembly circle; when the Vinaya-expert rises, the entire assembly rises. 'It should not be passed over' means the decision should not be allowed to lapse. 'A wrong path should not be followed' means an offense should not be revealed. 'One should not be reckless' means one should not act hastily; a harsh word should not be spoken hastily. This is the meaning. 'By one who is patient with speech' means by one who has the nature of enduring harsh speech. 'By one who seeks the welfare of the assembly' means by one who desires and seeks welfare; both compassion that has reached absorption and its preliminary stage should be established. This is the intention in this pair of words. 'By one who avoids ill-spoken words' means that ill-spoken speech, which is un-beautiful speech designated as contentious talk, should not be spoken. This is the meaning. 'Oneself should be assessed' means one should assess oneself thus: 'Will I be able to decide and to appease, or not?' One should know one's own measure. This is the meaning. 'The other should be assessed' means the other should be assessed thus: 'Is this assembly conscientious? Can they be made to understand, or not?'

Codako pariggahetabboti “dhammacodako nu kho no”ti evaṃ pariggahetabbo. Cuditako pariggahetabboti “dhammacuditako nu kho no”ti evaṃ pariggahetabbo. Adhammacodako pariggahetabboti [V 0.0210] tassa pamāṇaṃ jānitabbaṃ. Sesesupi eseva nayo. Vuttaṃ [M 0.0207] ahāpentenāti codakacuditakehi vuttavacanaṃ ahāpentena. Avuttaṃ apakāsentenāti anosaṭaṃ vatthuṃ appakāsentena. Mando hāsetabboti mando momūho paggaṇhitabbo, “nanu tvaṃ kulaputto”ti uttejetvā anuyogavattaṃ kathāpetvā tassa anuyogo gaṇhitabbo. Bhīrū assāsetabboti yassa saṅghamajjhaṃ vā gaṇamajjhaṃ vā anosaṭapubbattā sārajjaṃ uppajjati, tādiso “mā bhāyi, vissaṭṭho kathehi, mayaṃ te upatthambhā bhavissāmā”ti vatvāpi anuyogavattaṃ kathāpetabbo. Caṇḍo nisedhetabboti apasādetabbo tajjetabbo. Asuci vibhāvetabboti alajjiṃ pakāsetvā āpattiṃ desāpetabbo. Ujumaddavenāti yo bhikkhu uju sīlavā kāyavaṅkādirahito, so maddaveneva upacaritabbo. Dhammesu ca puggalesu cāti ettha yo dhammagaruko hoti na puggalagaruko, ayameva dhammesu ca puggalesu ca majjhattoti veditabbo.

The accuser should be examined thus: 'Is he an accuser according to the Dhamma or not?' The accused should be examined thus: 'Is he one accused according to the Dhamma or not?' Regarding an accuser not according to the Dhamma, his measure should be known. This same method applies in the remaining cases. 'Not diminishing what was said' means not diminishing the words spoken by the accuser and the accused. 'Not revealing what was unsaid' means not disclosing a matter that has not been introduced. A dull person should be encouraged—a dull, greatly bewildered person should be supported; having roused him by saying, 'Are you not a clansman?', and having made him state the procedure of interrogation, his statement should be accepted. A timid person should be reassured—if for someone timidity arises because of never before having entered the midst of the Saṅgha or a group, such a person should be told, 'Do not be afraid, speak confidently, we will be your support,' and then he should be made to state the procedure of interrogation. A harsh person should be restrained—he should be rebuked and threatened. An impure person should be exposed—the shameless one should be revealed, and he should be made to confess his offense. 'With straightforward gentleness'—a monk who is straightforward, virtuous, and free from bodily crookedness and the like should be treated only with gentleness. 'With regard to both the Dhamma and persons'—here, one who respects the Dhamma and not persons should be understood as being impartial with regard to both the Dhamma and persons.

366. Suttaṃ saṃsandanatthāyātiādīsu tena ca pana evaṃ sabrahmacārīnaṃ piyamanāpagarubhāvanīyena anuvijjakena samudāhaṭesu suttādīsu suttaṃ saṃsandanatthāya; āpattānāpattīnaṃ saṃsandanatthanti veditabbaṃ. Opammaṃ nidassanatthāyāti opammaṃ atthadassanatthāya. Attho viññāpanatthāyāti attho jānāpanatthāya. Paṭipucchā ṭhapanatthāyāti pucchā puggalassa ṭhapanatthāya. Okāsakammaṃ codanatthāyāti vatthunā vā āpattiyā vā codanatthāya. Codanā sāraṇatthāyāti dosādosaṃ sarāpanatthāya. Sāraṇā savacanīyatthāyāti dosādosasāraṇā savacanīyakaraṇatthāya. Savacanīyaṃ palibodhatthāyāti savacanīyaṃ “imamhā āvāsā paraṃ mā pakkamī”ti evaṃ palibodhatthāya. Palibodho vinicchayatthāyāti vinicchayaṃ pāpanatthāya. Vinicchayo santīraṇatthāyāti dosādosaṃ santīraṇatthāya tulanatthāya. Santīraṇaṃ ṭhānāṭṭhānagamanatthāyāti āpattianāpattigarukalahukāpattijānanatthāya[P 7.1366]. Saṅgho sampariggahasampaṭicchanatthāyāti vinicchayasampaṭiggahaṇatthāya ca; suvinicchitadubbinicchitabhāvajānanatthāya cāti attho. Paccekaṭṭhāyino avisaṃvādakaṭṭhāyinoti issariyādhipaccajeṭṭhakaṭṭhāne [M 0.0208] ca avisaṃvādakaṭṭhāne ca ṭhitā; na te apasādetabbāti attho.

366. In the phrases such as 'The Sutta is for the purpose of comparison': Furthermore, among the Suttas and so on brought forth in this way by that investigator who is dear, agreeable, respected, and esteemed by his companions in the holy life, the Sutta is for the purpose of comparison; it should be known that it is for comparing offenses and non-offenses. 'A simile is for the purpose of illustration' means a simile is for the purpose of showing the meaning. 'The meaning is for the purpose of making known' means the meaning is for the purpose of informing. 'A counter-question is for the purpose of establishing' means a question is for the purpose of establishing a person in one's decision. 'The act of asking for leave is for the purpose of accusation' means for the purpose of accusing by way of a matter or an offense. 'Accusation is for the purpose of reminding' means for the purpose of causing one to remember what is a fault and what is not a fault. 'Reminding is for the purpose of making one open to admonition' means causing one to remember faults and non-faults is for the purpose of making one liable to be spoken to. 'Being open to admonition is for the purpose of obstruction' means being liable to be spoken to is for the purpose of obstruction, thus: 'Do not go from this monastery to another.' 'Obstruction is for the purpose of a decision' means for the purpose of reaching a decision. 'A decision is for the purpose of investigation' means for the purpose of investigating and weighing what is a fault and what is not a fault. 'Investigation is for the purpose of arriving at what is and is not a proper case' means for the purpose of knowing an offense, a non-offense, a grave offense, and a light offense. 'The Saṅgha is for the purpose of complete acceptance and reception' means for the purpose of accepting the decision and for knowing the state of being well-decided or badly-decided. This is the meaning. 'Those standing apart, those standing without disagreement' means those standing in positions of authority, lordship, and seniority, and in positions of being undeceiving; they should not be rebuked. This is the meaning.

Idāni ye mandā mandabuddhino evaṃ vadeyyuṃ “vinayo nāma kimatthāyā”ti tesaṃ vacanokāsapidahanatthamattaṃ dassetuṃ vinayo saṃvaratthāyātiādimāha. Tattha vinayo saṃvaratthāyāti sakalāpi vinayapaññatti kāyavacīdvārasaṃvaratthāya. Ājīvavisuddhipariyosānassa sīlassa upanissayo [V 0.0211] hoti; paccayo hotīti attho. Esa nayo sabbattha. Apicettha avippaṭisāroti pāpapuññānaṃ katākatavasena cittavippaṭisārābhāvo. Pāmujjanti dubbalā taruṇapīti. Pītīti balavā bahalapīti. Passaddhīti kāyacittadarathapaṭippassaddhi. Sukhanti kāyikacetasikasukhaṃ. Tañhi duvidhampi samādhissa upanissayapaccayo hoti. Samādhīti cittekaggatā. Yathābhūtañāṇadassananti taruṇavipassanā; udayabbayañāṇassetaṃ adhivacanaṃ. Cittekaggatā hi taruṇavipassanāya upanissayapaccayo hoti. Nibbidāti sikhāpattā vuṭṭhānagāminibalavavipassanā. Virāgoti ariyamaggo. Vimuttīti arahattaphalaṃ. Catubbidhopi hi ariyamaggo arahattaphalassa upanissayapaccayo hoti. Vimuttiñāṇadassananti paccavekkhaṇāñāṇaṃ. Vimuttiñāṇadassanaṃ anupādāparinibbānatthāyāti apaccayaparinibbānatthāya. Apaccayaparinibbānassa hi taṃ paccayo hoti, tasmiṃ anuppatte avassaṃ parinibbāyitabbatoti. Etadatthā kathāti ayaṃ vinayakathā nāma etadatthā. Mantanāti vinayamantanā eva. Upanisāti ayaṃ “vinayo saṃvaratthāyā”tiādikā paramparapaccayatāpi etadatthāya. Sotāvadhānanti imissā paramparapaccayakathāya sotāvadhānaṃ. Imaṃ kathaṃ sutvā yaṃ uppajjati ñāṇaṃ, tampi etadatthāya. Yadidaṃ anupādā cittassa vimokkhoti yo ayaṃ catūhi upādānehi anupādiyitvā cittassa arahattaphalasaṅkhāto vimokkho, sopi etadatthāya; apaccayaparinibbānatthāya evāti attho.

Now, for those who, being dull and of feeble intellect, might say, 'For what purpose is the Vinaya?', to close off their opportunity for speech, the Venerable Upāli said, 'The Vinaya is for the sake of restraint,' and so on. Herein, 'The Vinaya is for the sake of restraint' means the entire Vinaya ruling is for the sake of restraint of the doors of body and speech. It is a strong supporting condition for virtue that culminates in purity of livelihood; this is the meaning. This is the method everywhere. Moreover, here 'non-regret' means the absence of mental regret by way of wholesome and unwholesome deeds done and not done. 'Gladness' is weak, tender rapture. 'Rapture' is strong, abundant rapture. 'Tranquility' is the calming of bodily and mental agitation. 'Happiness' is bodily and mental happiness. Indeed, both kinds are a strong supporting condition for concentration. 'Concentration' is one-pointedness of mind. 'Knowledge and vision of things as they really are' is tender insight; this is a designation for the knowledge of arising and passing away. Indeed, one-pointedness of mind is a strong supporting condition for tender insight. 'Disenchantment' is strong insight that has reached its peak and leads to emergence. 'Dispassion' is the Noble Path. 'Liberation' is the fruit of Arahantship. Indeed, the fourfold Noble Path is a strong supporting condition for the fruit of Arahantship. 'Knowledge and vision of liberation' is reviewing knowledge. 'Knowledge and vision of liberation is for the sake of final Nibbāna without clinging' means for the sake of Nibbāna without condition. Indeed, it is a condition for Nibbāna without condition, because upon having attained it, one must certainly attain final Nibbāna. 'This is the purpose of the discourse' means this discourse on the Vinaya has this purpose. 'Deliberation' is precisely the deliberation on the Vinaya. 'Foundation' means this state of being a sequential condition, beginning with 'The Vinaya is for the sake of restraint,' is also for this purpose. 'Attentive listening' is listening to this discourse on sequential conditions. The knowledge that arises upon hearing this discourse is also for this purpose. 'That which is the liberation of the mind without clinging' means: whatever liberation of the mind, called the fruit of Arahantship, there is, through not clinging with the four kinds of clinging, that too is for this purpose; it is only for the purpose of Nibbāna without condition. This is the meaning.

367. Anuyogavattagāthāsu [P 7.1367] paṭhamagāthā vuttatthā eva.

367. Among the verses on the procedure of interrogation, the first verse has the meaning already stated.

Vatthuṃ vipattiṃ āpattiṃ, nidānaṃ ākāraakovido pubbāparaṃ na jānātīti “vatthu”ntiādīni “na jānātī”ti padena sambandho. “Akovido”ti [M 0.0209] padassa “sa ve tādisako”ti iminā sambandho.
